     is dealt with by where it is, and committed: `pending` → ended at once `failed`/`interrupted`;
     `running`, its deploy step not begun (the cancel row) → asked to end itself
     `failed`/`interrupted` exactly as the cancel row asks, so it never reaches its deploy; its deploy
     step begun and neither its `deploy.completed` row written nor, under `run_by: org`, its `deploy`
     release sent → **a stop is recorded pending on the run**, who asked and when; its
     `deploy.completed` row written, or the run already ending — it read whether a stop was recorded
     on it, under this same lock, on its way to its end — → nothing on the run, and the undeploy stops
     what is there itself; under `run_by: org`, its `deploy` release sent and not yet reported → the
     stop is recorded on it as well, naming who asked, but nothing is pending: step 2's `remove`
     supersedes its release (below). When the run changed — ended, asked to end, a stop recorded —
     the same commit writes the audit row `request undeploy`, `detail{env, run}`; asking again, or
     about a run left as it was, writes none.
  2. With a stop pending, the route ends here: `202 {stopping: {run}}`, no `/stop`, and the run carries
     the stop out (below). Otherwise, when something is recorded there, `POST /stop` — a container
     already gone is a `204` (executor.md, invariant 6) — and the `remove` release when the org's
     platform runs the environment or has a release of it open (sent or accepted, newer than its row):
     both, when the org's platform is taking over an environment Canyon still runs; with nothing
     recorded there, only the run it ended or asked to end was writing it, nothing is stopped, and the
     audit row's `package` is `null`.
  3. Under the ledger lock: the row Canyon ran deleted — one the org runs left `pending` on the
     `remove`, as the route says — and the route's audit row written; `204`, or `202 {release}` when a
     `remove` was sent or an open one answered (answered again, no audit row). A
     `/stop` that fails is `500`: step 1's commit stays, the env's row stays, and a retry finds the
     run ended or asked and nothing left to do but the stop.
- **A stop pending on a run is carried out on every way that run ends** — its deploy step's
  transaction, a container that did not start, a step that errored, a replay after a crash (it reads
  the pending stop in that same transaction), the server-start reconcile ending a run DBOS gave up on
  (tried a few times there: an executor rolled with the server may not be up yet), and under `run_by:
  org` before its release is sent. Under the ledger lock it writes no
  `deploy.completed` row, no `deploy <env>` audit row, no `live at` line and no release — the env's row
  says what runs there until the stop lands; then it stops the environment as this route would, as a
  retried step — `/stop`, and a `remove` when the org's platform runs the environment or has a
  release of it open, this run's or an earlier one's, as step 2 sends; then it deletes the row Canyon
  ran (one the org runs is left `pending` on that `remove`), writes the route's audit
  row — `actor` the one who asked, `detail{env, package, run}`, `package` the run's, the one it was
  switching in — writes its line (events.md, *the brake's lines*) and ends `failed`/`interrupted`. A
  stop that fails past its retries leaves the env's row saying what runs there — the run writes its
  own row as its deploy would when its container started, else leaves the row as it was — and the run
  writes its line and ends `failed`/`error`, `failed_in: deploy`; the next undeploy finds the row and
  stops it. Asking again while the stop is pending answers the same `202`
  and writes nothing; the first who asked is kept.
- **Under `run_by: org`** the undeploy's `remove` supersedes any open `deploy` release of the
  environment: a run still delivering it stops retrying, and a run waiting on the org's report on it
  (the deploys row) ends at once — a remove of the environment newer than its release is what it
  reads — its line, then `failed`/`interrupted`, never waiting out its 30 minutes. An environment with
  an open `deploy` release and no row yet, the org's first deploy there, gets a `remove` naming that
  release's package and image. The org's report on a superseded release is `409 superseded` and writes
  nothing (the report row) but this: when it is a `deployed` on a `deploy` release, the environment's
  newest release is a `remove` newer than it, no deploy — Canyon's or the org's — has followed that
  `remove`, Canyon has run nothing there since the release, and no run is writing the environment now,
  the org's platform started what was taken offline, and Canyon sends another `remove` naming that
  release's own package and image — whether or not the first is still open — once: the same report
  again sends nothing more. It writes one audit row, actor Canyon, `send remove again`
  `detail{release, env, superseded}`, `release` the new remove, `superseded` the reported one. An
  environment a person has put back since is never touched. From the moment a `remove` is recorded the
  pods of every release of the environment recorded before it admit nobody: their access pull answers the
  deny-all file from then on, whatever is deployed after (the `GET /access/:app/:env` row; #2450), so pods
  the platform is slow to take down, or never does, let no one in.
- **What reads it.** No read carries a pending stop: the `202`, the run's timeline and the trail say
  it. An environment Canyon runs leaves `envs` — the project read, the status read, the fleet, and
  the receipt's preview — once the run carrying the stop out has begun ending (**amended 2026-10-11**,
  #2193: while Kubernetes waited for its pods to go, status read the version being taken offline as
  `stopped`, with a hint to read its logs), never reading `stopped` while it is taken offline; and,
  with no run carrying the stop out, while the undeploy's own `/stop` of it is under way (**amended
  2026-10-15**, #2202), or a hold's (#2262): the undeploy or the hold marks the row before it calls
  `/stop`, and the reads leave a marked row out only while the project's undeploy lock is held, and
  whoever takes that lock next clears a mark it did not set before its own work, so a stop that
  fails, or a server that died mid-stop, shows it again, never hidden by a later hold or undeploy; one
  the org's platform runs stays, with its pending remove. A release open on an environment left out still
  reads as its `pending`, `run_by: org`, as where no row is there: the brake's remove ends it. The row
  Canyon runs stays until the stop lands, so an undeploy and the server-start reconcile still find it,
  and a stop that fails shows it again once the run has ended; a run stuck while ending keeps the
  environment left out until it ends or the reconcile ends it. While a preview is taken offline the
  fleet reads its package's stage as the deploy run's, `building`. A run the brake ended reads
  `interrupted`, *it was cancelled, or another run of this app replaced it* (*A failure reason in
  words*): the undeploy is a cancel, and `request undeploy` names the run.

### The hold

**Amended 2026-10-11** (#2061; design/OPERATIONS.md §3.4–§3.9; design/PERMISSIONS.md, *Security
responders*): a responder or an admin stops an app's production with a reason and holds it, and every
production write is refused until a responder or an admin lifts it. One hold per app, in `prod_holds`:
what production ran — package, image, gate run and who deployed it, all `null` when it ran nothing —
the reason, who and when, and the app's team as it stood then: every owner and contributor grant's
address, bound or not; every subject one of those grants is bound to, or a session at one of those
addresses or in one of its owner or contributor groups carries; and those groups (the lift, below). A
sign-in at a recorded address or in a recorded group, verified or not, adds its subject to the record
in the sign-in's transaction, under the ledger lock the hold is placed under, as does a claim or a
share that binds a recorded address.

| Route | Request | Response |
|---|---|---|
| `POST /api/v1/projects/:id/envs/prod/hold` | `{reason}` — 1 to 1024 characters of plain text | **stop and hold**: minimum **a responder or an admin**, a person, from any session — never a level: an app's owner who is neither is refused like anyone · on one connection, under the undeploy lock throughout (*The brake*) and the ledger lock never across an executor call: (1) under the ledger lock the hold row is written, the run writing production is dealt with as *The brake*'s step 1 deals with it — writing no `request undeploy` row: the hold is the record — and every `pending` production approval of the app turns `outdated`, recording the hold's `by`, `at` and `reason` on it (*Production approvals*), committed together; (2) with a stop left pending on a run, **`202 {stopping: {run}}`**, and the run carries the stop out and writes the `stop app` row once its container has stopped (below); otherwise `POST /stop`, or the `remove` release when the org's platform runs production or has a release of it open; (3) under the ledger lock the row Canyon ran deleted and one audit row, **`stop app`** `detail{env, package?, image_digest?, reason, cancelled[]}` — `package` and `image_digest` what production ran, absent when it ran nothing, `cancelled` the run *The brake* ended or asked to end — then **`204`**, or **`202 {release}`** when a `remove` was sent (an open `remove` answered again writes no row) · production running nothing still takes a hold, `204` · a `/stop` that fails is `500`: the hold and step 1 stay, and the same request again resumes, writing the audit row once, with the first request's reason; asking again once production is stopped and held is `409 held` · refusals, in this order: `404 {error:"not_found"}` an unknown or deleted project; **`403 {error:"responder_required", message:"only a security responder or an admin can hold production. Its team takes it offline with canyon undeploy.", admins[]}`** — `admins` the org's admin addresses, the list and `CANYON_ADMINS` — to anyone neither, owners included; **`403 {error:"person_required"}`** to an `agent` or `canyon` actor, whatever the policies and the role: holding is a person's act; `400 {error:"invalid_reason"}`; `409 {error:"deleting"}` while the app is being deleted (the project delete row); `409 held` · each `403` writes one `denied` audit row · a wait on the app's undeploy lock — behind an undeploy, a hold, a delete or a recreation of its network (Egress control) — is bounded at 20 s: past it `409 {error:"busy", message}` and nothing is done, so no waiter holds a database connection without end (**amended 2026-10-15**, #2254) |
| `DELETE /api/v1/projects/:id/envs/prod/hold?held_at=&start=` | — | **lift**: a responder off the app's team, or an admin, from a **browser session only** — on a host with sign-in off, where no session tells the browser from the CLI, from any · **`204`** — the hold row deleted, production left empty until the team promotes, and one audit row, **`lift hold`** `detail{held_by, held_at, reason, started, package?}`, the actor the one lifting, `package` the held version it started, present when `started` (amended 2026-10-15, #2244: the trail names the version a lift started) · `start` exactly `true`: in the same transaction a promote run of the held image and gate run — the one production start a responder or an admin makes, the same bytes, promoted once already and not reviewed again — **`202 {run: {id, state:"pending"}}`** the promotions row's shape, `started` `true`; its production row names the deployer the held row named; that run's `deploy prod` audit row carries `restart: true` — under `run_by: org` the run sends a release and the org's report writes `platform reported deployed`, which carries none, so the `lift hold` row's `started: true` is the record of the restart; a hold that ran nothing is **`409 {error:"nothing_held", message:"production ran nothing when it was held: lift it without starting it"}`** · **the team** here is the owner and contributor grants, to the caller directly or through a group their session carries — a viewer grant or an `everyone` share is none — now, or on the hold's record: the caller's subject among its subjects, their address among its addresses, one of their groups among its groups — and, while the app has an owner or contributor group now or on the record, a session whose sign-in could not read the person's groups whole (an overage, a claim absent), which may be in one (design/PERMISSIONS.md) · refusals, in this order: `404 {error:"not_found"}` an unknown or deleted project; **`403 {error:"responder_required", message:"only a security responder who is not on this app's team, or an admin, can lift its hold.", admins[]}`** to anyone neither a responder nor an admin, **and to a responder on the team** — the role is read before the session, so an owner lifting from the CLI is `responder_required`; **`403 {error:"dashboard_required", message:"lift it in Canyon's dashboard"}`** to any session whose `flow` is not `browser`, with sign-in on; `409 {error:"undeploying"}` while an undeploy or a hold of the app is in progress; `404 {error:"not_found"}` no hold; **`held_at`** is the hold the caller read — its `at` exactly as the reads answer it — and the lift acts on that hold alone, so a lift asked from a stale read never lifts, or starts again, a hold placed since (amended 2026-10-14, #2069's review): absent or not an RFC 3339 instant `400 {error:"invalid_held_at"}`, another `409 {error:"hold_changed", message:"production's hold changed since you read it: read it again", hold:{by, at, reason}}`, the hold now, nothing written; `409 nothing_held`; with `start=true`, `409 deleting` while the app is being deleted and `409 run_in_progress` as the promotions row when a run of the app is `pending` or `running` — the hold stays · each `403` writes one `denied` audit row |

- **`409 held`** is `{error:"held", message, hold:{by, at, reason}, admins[]}` — `message` `Production
  was stopped by <who> on <date>: <reason>. It stays stopped until a responder or an admin lets it run
  again.` (design/EXPERIENCE.md D5), `<who>` the holder's display, else their subject, `<date>`
  `YYYY-MM-DD` UTC; `admins` as above. It answers every production write while production is held, an
  admin's too: a promotion and a rollback's (the promotions row), approving a production approval
  (which is the promotions route), undeploying `prod` (nothing runs there), deleting the app — once
  `confirm` names it: the `409 confirm_required` listing comes first — and the org's `deployed` report
  on production (the report row, whose `409 held` names no hold and no admins: the platform's token is
  no person's), all but the lift's own start. A claim of an ownerless app (`PUT …/owner`) is refused
  while it is held, as one that runs something is. The level or role is checked first, then the actor
  and the session, and only then the hold, so a caller refused for who they are — a viewer, a member
  with no access, a responder off the team — gets that refusal and never learns of a hold. Preview,
  checks and secrets go on.
- **Enforced in four places, and in no route of its own**: a start of a run that writes production
  (`runs.StartTx`, *The brake*'s run writing an environment) is refused, as an undeploy in progress
  refuses one, and the route answers `409 held`; a run that started before the hold is stopped before
  its deploy step, as a cancel stops it (the cancel row); the one production writer (the promote run's
  and the report's) writes nothing while held unless a stop is pending on its run — recorded on it and
  no `deploy` release of it sent — which then stops the container it started; and the receipt carries
  `held` (*The promote receipt*). A hold that cannot be read fails the transaction, so nothing starts.
- **A hold that met a promotion mid-switch** is carried out by that run as *The brake* says, and the
  run writes the hold's audit row: **`stop app`**, the actor the one who held, `detail{env, package,
  image_digest, reason, cancelled: [run]}`, under the same rule as the undeploy row it writes in its
  place (a row Canyon ran, or a `remove` sent) — and that undeploy row too only when someone other than
  the one who held asked the stop first. A stop the run cannot carry out while held leaves the hold, the
  run's last line is `production was held while <version> was starting; Canyon could not stop it:
  an admin can look up run <id>` in place of the brake's, and it writes the same `stop app` row with
  `stopped: false` — no row Canyon ran was deleted: it says what runs there. Under `run_by: org`, a held
  run that had handed its release to the platform ends with a `remove` of that release outstanding: one
  open already, or a new one, since the platform may have started it after the hold's `remove` completed. The hold row keeps what
  production ran before the promotion — the image a lift's `start=true` starts again — while the run's
  `stop app` row names the package and image it was switching in, which is what was stopped.
- **Reads**: `hold` `{by, at, reason, version}` — `version` what production ran, named as *Naming a
  version* says, `null` when nothing — on the project read, the fleet row and the status read, to
  contributors and above, responders and admins; never on a reduced row but a responder's (the project
  read). The fleet's `held=true` lists the held apps among them. Production's row is gone once stopped,
  so a held production has no `envs.prod`; a stop still pending on a run is no read's (*The brake*).
- **Under hand-off** (amended 2026-10-24, #2450) production admits nobody from the moment of the hold,
  though the platform has not yet taken its pods down: production's access pull answers the deny-all file
  (the `GET /access/:app/:env` row) while the hold stands, and the hold's `remove` keeps the pods of every
  release before it stopped after the lift too; only a new release's pods pull the list. Preview's pull is
  unchanged.
- **The image is kept** while held, so `start=true` starts its exact bytes (executor.md, `keep`).
- **While held, a deploy run of the app** (a preview deploy) writes the hold's line as its last
  `build.log` row before its terminal state (events.md), so the builder reads it after `Live`.
- **During a rolling upgrade** a replica still on the binary before the hold enforces none of it, matches
  a grant by its address or its subject, records an unverified person's subject on an access request,
  and rebinds a grant on a claim (design/OPERATIONS.md §3.4): hold, and bind, once every replica runs it.

### Patch runs

**Added 2026-10-18** (#2086; design/PATCHING.md §4): Canyon moves an app onto a newer run image under
its unchanged layers, through the gate a person's deploy passes, to preview on its own and to
production only when a person approves (*Production approvals*). Nothing here starts while the org's
`patching` reads `off` (the preferences row), nor until the operator turns patching on: `CANYON_PATCHING`
`on`, unset `off`, any other value refusing the server's start (beta; amended 2026-10-19, #2098), nor on a server
built without Canyon Enterprise, which reads `patching` as `off` whatever it stores (*Licence*; amended 2026-11-15,
#2923). Off, each
rescan tick still reads and matches the images that run, so the receipt reads them as before, and no patch
run is planned or starts; one queued before ends at step 1, `failed`/`interrupted`. The sample is `contracts/testdata/patch/claims-portal.json`
(patch.schema.json): a patch that passed its watch, and one that was reverted.

- **A patch run** is a run of the project, `kind: "patch"` on its rows (the gate-runs rows; events.md's
  `run.transition`), `started_by` `{kind: "canyon", subject: "patch", display: "Canyon"}`, on the
  package preview runs, on the server's `patches` queue, started only while no other run of the
  project is queued or running, one at a time with them. It writes preview. A patch reaches production
  only through a promote run that re-`/run`s the image that served preview (executor.md, invariant 4): a
  patch approval's, or a person's promotion of the patched preview, which writes `patch prod` and marks
  production's history `patched` as a patch approval's does (amended 2026-10-20, #2098). The mark is the
  image's: a rollback to an image a patch made — a `(package, image)` production's history already marks
  `patched` — is a person's `deploy prod` with `rollback: true` and `patched: true` in its `detail`, never
  `patch prod`, and its history row is marked `patched` too, so `earlier[]` never reads it unpatched once
  production moves on (amended 2026-10-28, #2390). The gate-runs rate limit neither counts nor limits it, as a promotion.
- **Its author is the author of the run whose image it rebases** — preview's row's `gate_run`, a patch
  run's own author when that run was a patch — for every rule that never lets a run's author approve
  its findings (the approve rows; design/PERMISSIONS.md, *Approve findings*): the approve routes'
  `author_cannot_approve`, `approvable`, and what an approval covers wherever one is read (runs'
  `CoversBlocking`). A run whose image's run recorded no author records none, and then no approval
  covers its blocking findings (fail closed). `started_by` stays Canyon's: it says who started the
  run, never whose code it is. The run records it as it is enqueued, and the approve routes and
  `CoversBlocking` read it (amended 2026-10-19, #2098) — so no one approves, through a patch run, a
  finding of code they shipped.
- **A person's run goes first.** Any start of another run of the project — a deploy, a GitHub push's
  among them, a gate run, a promotion — ends an active patch run `failed`/`interrupted` at any step
  before its `/run`, as a pending run is ended, and goes ahead — a lift's start of the held image (*The
  hold*) among them; so do a delete of the project and a recreation of its network (*Egress control*),
  and an undeploy (*The brake*) or a hold (*The hold*) does so only to a patch run of the environment it
  takes offline. During the `/run`
  itself, the executor's settle window, a start is `409 {error:"run_in_progress", run}` (the gate-runs
  row); after it, a request that changes what runs ends the watch clean (below) and goes ahead — the
  start of a run that deploys (a deploy, a GitHub push's among them, a promotion, a rollback's review), a
  delete of the project, an undeploy or a hold. The start of a run that deploys nothing — a check, a pull
  request's — leaves the watch running and is `409 {error:"run_in_progress", run, watch}` while it runs,
  `watch` `{until}` its `watch_until` (the gate-runs row; amended 2026-10-29, #2568), as during the `/run`; so is a recreation of its network, which starts no run (amended 2026-10-29, #2100). From
  the moment the run's revert takes the undeploy lock (below) until it ends, a start is
  `409 {error:"run_in_progress", run}` too, never `undeploying`. The same holds of a promote
  run shipping a patch while it watches production. Its cancel is the cancel row's.
- **Its steps**, in this order on its timeline (events.md), each ending the run with nothing deployed
  when it fails, its line then the last `build.log` row:
  1. **The gate**, as the deploys row runs it on that package: today's snapshot, every tool, the
     review cache, the project's unexpired approvals. `blocked` ends the run `blocked`.
  2. **`rebase`**: executor.md's `POST /rebase` of the image preview runs onto the run image the plan
     named. A refusal or a failure ends it `failed`/`error` (amended 2026-10-19, #2098): `run_image_moved`
     with line *moved*; `not_rebasable`, `another_run_image` or `another_stack` with line *refused*, the
     pair never planned again (design/PATCHING.md §4.2); a `404`, a `502` or an executor out of reach with
     line *rebase-failed*.
  3. **`image-sbom`**: the rebased image's SBOM (executor.md, `/scan` `target: "image"`), stored as a
     deploy's is, then the covered layers of it and of the image it replaces matched at the step, on the
     executor's database, and both read as the receipt reads them (amended 2026-10-20, #2098). A scan or a
     match that failed or did not finish, a read that did not check, the two read on different databases,
     or a database past the 5-day limit, is never zero findings: `failed`/`error`, line *unchecked* —
     `<reason>` the rebased image's read's `why` (*The promote receipt*, `platform.known-vuln`'s row), else
     `could not check: its image scan did not finish` when its scan fails, `could not check:
     its last rescan did not finish` when a match fails, and `could not check: the two images were
     matched on different databases` when their layers name two database builds — or line *unread* when the
     replaced image's read did not check, `<reason>` its `why`.
  4. **The proof**, from the rebase's `before` and `after` facts: the diff IDs above `top_layer`, in
     order, `build_metadata` and `config` the same, on the same `stack_id`, `top_layer` moved, and
     `after`'s `run_image` the planned run image — the same digest as the `GET /builder` answer the plan
     named (the identity, Platform). Then line *proof*. A fact that differs or is missing is
     `failed`/`not_platform_only` with `not_platform_only: "layers_changed"` and line *layers*; another
     run image under the image is `failed`/`error` and line *moved*.
  5. **The run-config fingerprint**: a sha256 the deploy row records as it is written, over what that
     row's `/run` carried beside its image (amended 2026-10-19, #2094): the egress spec the row records,
     whole — `{via}` (design/EGRESS.md §2.5; an app env's mode is live policy egress-control applies
     with no `/run`, so none is pending) — its `run_by`, the front door on Kubernetes as it was saved
     (executor.md, *The front door*): the saved door and when it was saved, never choice 2's client
     secret, a new one of which is a new save — the environment's runtime secrets' names, versions and
     set times, read with the values that `/run` was handed (a secret removed and set again starts at
     version 1 again; its set time moves on every write, a key rotation aside), and its resources'
     names. Beside the sha256 the row records those parts — names, versions, times, `via`, `run_by` and
     the door, never a value — so a fingerprint that differs names what changed; both the server's
     alone, answered by no read. What the platform hands every `/run` is outside it — the egress proxy
     (`CANYON_EGRESS_PROXY`), the roots of `egress.ca`, the database CA, the identity's issuer and key set: a patch's `/run`
     carries the host's current ones, as any `/run` does. A row the org's report writes records none,
     and so does one a retry writes on finding its version already running: what an earlier attempt's
     `/run` carried is not known. Recreate networks, which `/run`s each environment again, records that
     `/run`'s. A row a server from before the fingerprint deploys again, or recreates, records none:
     the database clears what it would leave stale (amended 2026-10-19, review of #2373). The fingerprint the
     patch's `/run` would carry must equal the row's: then line *unchanged*; otherwise, or on a row
     written before fingerprints, which records none, `failed`/`not_platform_only` with
     `not_platform_only: "config_changed"` and line *changed*. A patch never carries a pending
     configuration change into a running app. Its `/run` carries the egress spec the row records, never
     the host's now, and compares its fingerprint again as it builds what the `/run` carries, before the
     executor is touched: one changed since this step fails so, its line *changed* after *unchanged*
     (amended 2026-10-19, #2098). Equality is on the row's stored sha256; its parts only name what changed.
  6. **Regression**, read from the two images' reads of step 3, on one database (amended 2026-10-20,
     #2098), a finding its package and vulnerability: no
     counted finding (finding.md, *System libraries and build tools*) at or above `high` that the replaced image lacks — else
     `failed`/`patch_regressed` and line *regressed* — and at least one finding the plan named gone —
     else `failed`/`patch_ineffective` and line *ineffective*. Then line *fixed* once per package of a finding
     the plan named that it no longer has, in the plan's order, `<vulnerability>` each of that package's
     such findings, in the plan's order, joined by `, `, and `<to>` the version of the package, by name and
     class, the patched image's SBOM of step 3 holds — its versions joined by `, ` when it holds several,
     `removed` when it holds none — never the version the advisory fixes it in (amended 2026-11-03, #2631),
     and line *no-new*.
  7. **The deploy**: `/run` of the rebased image with `keep` holding the image it replaces (executor.md,
     *What `keep` spares*; design/OPERATIONS.md §1.5), its `start` step and `deploy.completed` row as a
     deploy run's — never the deploy run's access lines nor the hold's line (events.md) — and one audit
     row, actor Canyon, `patch preview` `detail{package, run, replaces, image, proof, fixed[]}` —
     `replaces` and `image` the two digests, `proof` `layers`, each of `fixed` `{name, from, to,
     vulnerability}`, one per finding, `to` as line *fixed* gives it: names and versions, never content. A promote run shipping a patch writes `patch
     prod` in place of `deploy prod`.
  8. **The watch**, `watch` (built 2026-10-29, #2100) — `CANYON_PATCH_WATCH` from the `/run`, its end
     journaled as `watch_until` so a resumed run waits for the same instant, and line *watching*. It
     reads the patched container's state every 30 s with executor.md's `POST /running`, asking of its one
     container (`GET /running` answers no restart: amended 2026-10-29, #2100); its one signal is an exit or
     a restart of the patched container — in `restarting` a restart, in neither `running` nor
     `restarting` an exit. A read that fails is read again at the next tick, as a restart is counted from
     the `/run` and an exit stays: only the read at `watch_until` must answer, else the watch is blind
     (below).
- **The revert.** On an exit or a restart in the watch the run marks itself reverting by
  compare-and-set under the ledger lock, so a request and a revert never both `/run`. It is a
  production writer as *The brake* has one: it takes the project's undeploy lock across its checks and
  its `/run`, so a hold or an undeploy that lands meanwhile waits for it, then stops what it put back.
  In one transaction it reads the hold again and checks that the environment's row is still the
  patch's, then `/run`s the kept image. Then the row is the one the patch replaced — its `image_digest`,
  `gate_run` and fingerprint as they were before the patch — no `deploy.completed` row is written, the
  audit row is `revert patch preview` or `revert patch prod`, actor Canyon, with the patch's row's
  detail, line *reverted*, and the run ends `failed`/`reverted`. A row that is no longer the patch's —
  a person's deploy got there first — is left as it is: line *superseded*, and the run ends `clean`. A
  kept image the `/run` answers missing (`404`), or a `/run` that fails, leaves the patched app running,
  its row the patch's: line *revert-failed*, and the run ends `failed`/`error`. A revert is not a
  rollback (design/OPERATIONS.md §0): it puts back the image that served before Canyon's own change,
  never an older version. Reverting is the run row's own mark, never a run state.
- **A watch ends clean** — no revert, the kept image released — at its end, line *passed*, or on a
  person's request, a stop, an undeploy or a hold, line *ended*; the run then ends `clean` and its
  `watch` step `done`. Only a watch that ran to `watch_until` counts as passed: one a request ended
  early files no patch approval and moves no wave on (the Platform rows). A watch that cannot read the
  container's state does not pass: nothing is reverted blind, nothing goes to production, the kept
  image stays kept while the environment's row is the patch's (amended 2026-10-29, #2100), line *blind*, and the run ends
  `failed`/`error`.
- **A patch run's `error` carries no `failed_in`**: its line says what failed.
- **Its lines**, byte for byte — each `build.log` row a patch run writes is one of these, but a rebuild patch's build output (*A rebuild patch*, step 2), `<version>`
  named as *Naming a version* says, `<HH:MM>` UTC, `<env>` `preview` or `production`, `<who>` the
  actor's `display`, else their `subject` (proof, layers, refused and moved **amended 2026-11-01**, #2605: they
  said `run image`, Canyon's own name for the system libraries under an app, which a reader does not know; proof,
  layers' `<what>`, refused, unchecked and unread **amended 2026-11-02**, #2614: they still said layers, the stack,
  the executor's refusal and the scanner's name):
  - proof: `Only the system libraries Canyon adds changed: <version>'s own code, its build settings and how it starts are the same`
  - layers: `More than Canyon's system libraries changed: <what> changed too, so Canyon did not deploy it` — `<what>` the first that differs of `the app's own files`, `the build settings`, `how the app starts` and `the Ubuntu release it's built on`
  - refused: `Canyon can't patch <version><why>, so its next deploy picks up the fix` — `<why>` by the `409`'s `error`: `: how it was built doesn't let Canyon swap in new system libraries` for `not_rebasable`, `: it was built on different system libraries from the ones Canyon fixes` for `another_run_image`, `: it was built on another Ubuntu release` for `another_stack`, and nothing for any other; the `409`'s `message`, which names the label, run image or stack, goes to the server log alone
  - unrecorded: `Preview runs the patched <version>, but Canyon could not record it: deploy again so Canyon records what runs there` — a patch whose `/run` succeeded and whose record failed past its retries, or whose `/run` began before a restart and the executor cannot say what preview runs (amended 2026-10-20, review of #2098: a `/run` begun is never read as not run, and a recovery records the rebased image it finds running); the cause is logged under the run's id
  - unfinished: `Canyon could not finish patching <version>, so it did not deploy it` — Canyon's own failure in any step, or the executor's in the `/run`; the cause is logged under the run's id alone
  - rebase-failed: `Canyon could not apply its fix to <version>: <error>, so it did not deploy it` (reworded 2026-11-01, #2079: it said `rebase`) — `<error>` the executor's error as Canyon got it, quoted as a scheduler's message is (finding.md, *Canyon-run tools*)
  - moved: `A newer fix from Canyon came out while it patched <version>, so Canyon did not deploy it: Canyon tries again at its next check`
  - unchecked: `Canyon could not check the patched image for vulnerabilities: <reason>, so it did not deploy it` — `<reason>` as step 3 gives it, after `could not check: `
  - unread: `Canyon could not check the image <version> runs for vulnerabilities: <reason>, so it did not deploy the patch` — `<reason>` the replaced image's read's `why`, after `could not check: `
  - unchanged: `What <version> runs with is unchanged since it was deployed`
  - changed: `<what> changed since <version> was deployed: deploy again to apply it, then Canyon patches.` — `<what>` the first that changed of `Its outbound setting`, `Who runs it`, `Its front door`, `The secret <NAME>` and `The resource <name>`, and `Its configuration` on a row with none
  - regressed: `The patched image has <name> <installed> (<vulnerability>, <severity>), which <version> did not: Canyon did not deploy it`
  - ineffective: `The patched image still has every package the patch was for: Canyon did not deploy it`
  - fixed: `Fixed: <name> <from> → <to> (<vulnerability>)`
  - no-new: `No new vulnerability at high or above`
  - watching: `Watching <env> until <HH:MM> UTC: if the patched app exits or restarts, Canyon puts back the image it replaced`
  - passed: `The watch passed: the patched app neither exited nor restarted`
  - ended: `The watch ended early: <who> <how>` — `<how>` `started another run of this app`, `took <env> offline` or `stopped production`
  - blind: `Canyon could not tell whether the patched app runs, so the patch goes no further: it runs in <env> as deployed`
  - reverted: `Canyon reverted <env> at <HH:MM> UTC: the patched app <how>, so the image it replaced runs again` — `<how>` `exited` or `restarted`
  - superseded: `The watch ended: <env> runs another version now, so nothing was put back`
  - revert-failed: `Canyon could not put back the image the patch replaced: <reason>. The patched image stays in <env>: deploy again to replace it` — `<reason>` `it is no longer on this Canyon` (the `/run` answered `404`), `it did not stay up` (it exited before it answered), `Canyon did not record which image it was`, else `Canyon couldn't put the old image back`; the cause is logged under the run's id (amended 2026-10-29, #2100: never the executor's words, and never that the patched app runs after it exited)
  - same: `Rebuilt with <newer>: <version>'s code, build settings and dependencies are the same` — a rebuild patch's (*A rebuild patch*), `<newer>` from the findings it fixed, those the plan named when it fixed none: `a newer Node.js`, `a newer Python`, `a newer Java` or `a newer Go` when the buildpack that fixes each — the one whose layer holds it, go-dist's for Go's standard library in the app's own build — is that one runtime's (`paketo-buildpacks/node-engine`, `cpython`, `bellsoft-liberica`, `go-dist`), else `newer build tools`: the app launcher's, a helper's or two runtimes' fixes, never a guess (amended 2026-11-02, #2619's review)
  - not-same: `<what> changed when Canyon rebuilt <version>, so it did not deploy it` — `<what>` `Its code` (`package`), `Its build settings` (`values`) or `How it is built` (`buildpacks`)
  - not-pinned: `Its dependencies changed when Canyon rebuilt <version> (<change>), so it did not deploy it: pin them in a lockfile, then deploy again` — `<change>` the first package, by type, name, version and purl in that order, that one build has and the other lacks, `<name> <was> → <now>`, `none` on the side that lacks it
  - rotated: `Canyon can't compare <version>'s build settings: Canyon's key for build settings changed since <version> was built, so it did not deploy the rebuild`
  - build-failed: `Canyon could not rebuild <version>: <error>, so it did not deploy it` — `<error>` the build's own last line, quoted as a scheduler's message is; Canyon's own failure in the build, or the executor's, is line *unfinished*, its cause logged under the run's id
  - rebuild-ineffective: `Canyon rebuilt <version> with <newer>, but the rebuild fixes none of its findings, so it did not deploy it`
- **A rebuild patch** (added 2026-11-02, #2111, #2117; design/PATCHING.md §5) is a patch run that builds the package
  preview runs again on the builder Canyon names now, for what a rebase cannot fix: a counted finding (finding.md,
  *System libraries and build tools*) of class `runtime` or `lifecycle`. It is never called platform-only, and it is **always asked**:
  - **Opt-in.** It is planned only while the org's `rebuilds` reads `ask` (the preferences row), each rescan tick after
    the rebase plan, for an app the tick planned no rebase for, whose preview runs an image named by digest with a
    counted finding of class `runtime` or `lifecycle` at or above `high`, whose image's build recorded, by digest, the
    builder that built it (`builder`, executor.md's `GET /builder` as that build asked it: the run that built the image's,
    else, for a rebased image no run built, its deploy row's) other than the one `GET /builder` names now, and whose row
    records a values-only HMAC. An image whose build recorded neither — built before them, or by a release before them
    — is never planned: it moves by its next deploy. A tick that plans no rebuild unplans every app a rebuild identity
    planned. Its identity (the Platform rows) is `kind: "rebuild"`, `id` the digest of
    the builder `GET /builder` names, `builder` that answer, and its waves, breaker, watch, revert, re-filing and every
    other rule of a patch run are a rebase's. At most one rebuild patch run of the host is before its `/run` at once —
    the plan starts none while one is queued or has not begun its `/run`; its watch holds nothing — and its build
    takes a slot of `CANYON_BUILD_CONCURRENCY` as a deploy's does. Its plan's causes are its own server log line, `runs: the rebuild plan onto <builder> started <n>
    rebuild(s) (<apps>)`, beside the rebase plan's; the identities read's `plan` stays the rebase plan's.
  - **Its steps** in place of steps 2 and 4, the rest as above: 2. **`build`**: the deploys row's build of the package
    preview runs, with preview's build values, never reusing an image, its `build.log` rows as a deploy's build
    writes them; one that does not build ends it `failed`/`error`, line *build-failed*, Canyon's own failure in it
    or the executor's line *unfinished*, and a build on another builder than the identity's ends it `failed`/`error`, line *moved*. 4. **`same_inputs`**,
    from what the two builds recorded, in this order, the first that differs ending it `failed`/`not_same_inputs`
    with `not_same_inputs` naming it: the same package and its tree hash (`package`) — a package's lockfiles are in
    its tree, so the tree hash is their digest; the values-only HMAC under the same key version, a row whose
    version is another not compared at all (`key_rotated`, line *rotated*) and one whose HMAC differs or is missing
    (`values`); the buildpack ids that ran, in order, their versions aside (`buildpacks`); and the two images' own
    packages — every library in their SBOMs but those of class `os`, `runtime` and `lifecycle`, so class `app` and
    `unknown`, what a go build, NuGet or another buildpack put there — each by type, name, version and purl, the same
    many times over (`dependencies`,
    line *not-pinned*: a `requirements.txt` that pins nothing resolves anew); the others line *not-same*. An image
    whose SBOM does not read is `dependencies` too. Then line *same*. Step 6's *ineffective* is line
    *rebuild-ineffective*.
  - **What it records.** Preview's row takes the build's build digest, values-only HMAC, key version, buildpacks and
    builder, as a deploy's does: a build made the image. The audit row `patch preview` carries `proof` `same_inputs`
    and `same_inputs` `{values_key, buildpacks}` — the key version both builds' values were compared under, and the
    buildpack ids, in order — and each of `fixed` carries its `class` too.
  - **Production is always asked.** Its patch approval reads `rebuild: true` and its own `proof` (*Production
    approvals*), and the fleet approval never decides it: an identity of `kind` `rebuild` refuses one, `409
    always_asked`.
- **The fleet's `gate_state`, the stage and the receipt.** A patch run is never the project's or the
  fleet's `gate_state`, the package's stage reads none (*The package's stage*), and a held patch never
  reads `failed` on the fleet. It is the status read's `promote_run`, and the receipt's newest run of
  the package, only while preview runs the image it deployed.
- **What a patch leaves on the environment's row**: its `package`, `label` and `deployed_by` stay; its
  `image_digest`, `gate_run` — the patch run — and fingerprint are the patch's; a rebase's keeps the values-only HMAC
  and the builder, a rebuild's takes its build's (*A rebuild patch*). Production's history
  marks the image `patched` (the status row's `earlier[]`).
- **`envs.<env>.patch`** (design/PATCHING.md §0.3), on the project read and so on the status read, at
  contributor and above and on no reduced row: `{state, since, identity?, run?, approval?, why?}`
  (patch.schema.json's `env_patch`) — `state` ∈ `none | planned | wait | patched_preview | asked |
  patched | reverted | held | denied | could_not_rescan`, `since` when it took that state, `identity` the
  platform identity a plan names (the Platform rows), `run` its newest patch run, `approval` the open
  patch approval's code while `asked` and the denied one's while `denied` (amended 2026-10-31, #2601), and `why`, exactly while `held`, one of `blocked` (the gate),
  `layers_changed`, `config_changed`, `not_same_inputs` (a rebuild's, *A rebuild patch*; added 2026-11-02, #2117), `patch_regressed`, `patch_ineffective`, `failed` (any other
  failure), `production_held`, `paused` (its identity), `preview_ahead` (*Production approvals*).
  Below contributor the environment carries none, and so does every read until the operator turns patching on
  (`CANYON_PATCHING`, beta): `envs.<env>.patch` is absent then, never `none`. **How each is read** (amended
  2026-10-29, #2105): from
  what Canyon recorded, on every read that carries the environment, the fleet's rows among them. `none` while
  Canyon does not patch the app (the preferences row's one condition) or records no patch of it. In
  preview: `planned` while its newest plan's patch run is queued, waiting on its wave, or under way before
  its deploy, or a request ended its watch early, so it is planned again; `patched_preview` while
  preview's row is a patch run's, its watch running or passed; `reverted` once its watch saw the patched
  app exit or restart and the image it replaced runs again; `held` when its newest patch run ended
  without deploying — `why` `blocked` for a gate that blocked, `layers_changed` or `config_changed` as
  its `not_platform_only`, `not_same_inputs`, `patch_regressed`, `patch_ineffective`, and `failed` for any other end or a
  start that did not stay up — or its identity is paused (`paused`). A rebase refused for good (*Patch
  runs*, step 2) reads `none`: the app moves by its next deploy. In production: `asked` while a patch
  approval of it is open — `pending`, unexpired and current, as its read reads it (*Production
  approvals*); `planned` while the promote run an approved patch approval started is under way or
  watching; `reverted` once that watch saw the patched app exit or restart, its `run` that promote run, whose
  timeline carries production's revert line (none where no approval names it); `held` while preview runs a
  patch whose watch passed and production cannot take it — production held (`production_held`), running
  another version than the patch replaced (`preview_ahead`), or the identity paused (`paused`); `denied`
  (amended 2026-10-31, #2601) while the app's newest patch approval was denied and production still runs the
  image it would have replaced, so it keeps what the patch fixes, and Canyon files none again for that identity
  (*Production approvals*), its `identity` and `run` that approval's, `since` its `decided_at`; and
  `patched` while production's row runs an image production's history marks `patched` and nothing above
  holds. `since` is when that fact was recorded: the plan's or the patch's newest outcome, the patch
  run's deploy while its watch runs, the approval's `requested_at` or `decided_at`, production's
  `deployed_at`, the hold's or the pause's time; for `none`, `wait` and `could_not_rescan`, the
  environment's `deployed_at`. **`wait` and `could_not_rescan` are read on the status read alone**: each
  needs the environment's image read (*System libraries and build tools*, `GET …/envs/:env/image`) and the match of the run
  image Canyon names, which neither the fleet nor the project read makes; there an environment that would
  read either reads `none`, never a claim that nothing waits. On the status read an environment that reads
  `none` while Canyon patches the app is read again: `could_not_rescan` when its image read could not
  check, or when it has a counted `os` finding at or above `high` and the run image Canyon names — the one
  the newest rescan tick read from executor.md's `GET /builder` — has no match that reads, or its match did
  not match that package, which is never read as `wait`; `wait` when such a finding is fixed in no version that run image carries
  (design/PATCHING.md §0.4); `none` otherwise.

### The promote receipt

One shape, at rest on the status read and inside the promote refusal, so the receipt on the page
and the receipt that refused are the same thing: `{org, blocked_by[], rules[], waiting?, held?}`.

- **While production is held it is not evaluated** (*The hold*): `held` is `{by, at, reason}`, the
  hold's, `blocked_by` is `["prod.held"]` alone, `rules` is empty, and `waiting` is never evaluated: the
  CLI, the status read, `--format ai` and the disabled Promote button all say why from this one field.

**Amended 2026-10-01** (`canyonv2-5r11.20`, audit C1, C2, C12): the receipt answers whether *this
preview* can be promoted, never how the app is doing, so it reads as nothing else.
- **With nothing running in preview it is not evaluated**: `rules` and `blocked_by` are empty and
  `waiting` is the one line to show — an app live in production with an empty preview is not failing
  a check. It names why nothing is there when the run the receipt is read for says (**amended
  2026-10-04**, #1520: deploying again would only repeat the block or the failure):
  - `Nothing is in preview: <version> was blocked at review. Fix the finding, or have an admin or a
    security responder who didn't start this review approve it with a reason, then deploy.` when that
    run ended `blocked` with a blocking finding no unexpired approval covers, `the finding` and `it`
    read `the findings` and `them` past one — who approves named as a blocking finding's `fix` names
    them (contracts/finding.md; amended 2026-10-14, #2237: it told a member to approve);
  - `Nothing is in preview: the build of <version> failed.` when it ended `failed`/`build`;
  - `Nothing is in preview: <version> didn't stay up after it started.` when it ended `failed`/`start`;
  - `Nothing is in preview: this host can't build <version>'s kind of app.` when it ended `failed`/`detect`;
  - `Nothing is in preview: the cluster had no room to build <version>.` when it ended `failed`/`no_room` (#1637);
  - `Nothing is in preview: the cluster had no room to review <version>. Deploy it again once it has.` when it ended `failed`/`scan_no_room` (**amended 2026-10-08**);
  - else `Deploy a new version to preview to promote it.` `waiting` is absent on every other
  receipt. A promotion asked then is `409 {error:"nothing_in_preview", message, receipt}`, that
  receipt, and changes nothing.
- **A preview Canyon runs that isn't running is waiting too** (**amended 2026-10-04**, #1557): a
  promotion is never made from a preview that isn't running. When preview is an environment Canyon
  runs (`run_by: canyon`) and its `state` reads `restarting`, `stopped` or `unknown` — asked of the
  executor on that read, as `envs.<env>.state` says — the receipt is not evaluated, as with nothing in
  preview, and `waiting` is:
  - `<version> restarted <n> times since it was deployed; deploy again once it's fixed (the build is reused).` when it reads `restarting`, `once` for one restart, `<n> times` dropped when the read carries no count — the way out, since one restart holds the promotion until the next deploy (**amended
    2026-10-05**, #1722: a preview that keeps exiting is never promoted from one of its up-windows);
  - `<version> isn't running in preview: canyon logs says why.` when it reads `stopped`;
  - `Canyon couldn't check whether <version> runs in preview.` when it reads `unknown`.

  The promote route asks the executor once for preview's state, before it reads under its lock, and
  a promotion asked then is the same `409 {error:"nothing_in_preview", message, receipt}`, `message`
  that line. A preview the org runs carries no `state` and never waits on it.
- **`prod.human-approval` states its rule**, whoever is reading, and the org's `prod_approval`
  (Platform, the preferences row) is that rule (amended 2026-10-07, #1820: the line said `agents
  can't`, and an agent holding its person's CLI session promoted). **Off**: `why` is
  `a person promotes, or an agent they ask, with their CLI sign-in`, and `passed` is whether the
  actor reading or asking is a person — a CLI session is its person. **On**: `why` is
  `a person approves it in Canyon's dashboard; the CLI only asks`, `passed` is whether the actor is a
  person on a browser session (`flow: browser`) — or, for a patch approval the fleet approval decides, the
  admin who gave it in the browser (the Platform rows; amended 2026-10-29, #2571) — and the rule blocks whatever its policy state, so
  the CLI's receipt reads it failed and the promotions route answers the CLI with a production
  approval rather than a refusal.
- **An advisory rule that did not pass shows an amber `!`**, never a ✗: a ✗ is a rule that stops
  the promotion. A rule that blocks and did not pass is the ✗.
- An unset `stack.blessed`'s `why` is `no approved stacks set` — the list is what is missing, not
  the rule's state, which an admin may well have set. The list is the preferences' `approved_buildpacks`.

- **A rollback review's receipt** (**amended 2026-10-11**, #2055; design/OPERATIONS.md §1): evaluated
  for a run the gate-runs row's `rollback_to` started — by `?run=` on the status read, or a promotion
  naming it — the receipt answers whether *that run* can be shipped, so preview is not read: `waiting`
  is never evaluated, and `prod.clean-gate` makes no preview check; past the run's own checks it fails
  when the run recorded no `ships` — its findings approved only after it ended — `why` `<version>'s
  review recorded no image to put back; review it again`; when a newer gate run of the package exists,
  a rollback review or not — a patch run counting only while preview runs the image it deployed (*Patch
  runs*) — `why` `<version> has a newer review than the one named`; and when the run
  has shipped once already, `why` `<version>'s review has put it back already; review it again`.
  `stack.blessed` reads the buildpacks of the build of the image it ships, and a placed rule's row reads
  the run as `prod.clean-gate` does. The receipt then carries **`ships {version, image_digest}`**: the
  version and the image a promotion of the run ships, once the run recorded it; no other receipt
  carries it.
- `org` is the org's display name, **the server's** (decided 2026-09-15; `Meridian` on the demo
  host) — server configuration the promote PR adds; nothing stores it today. D5's line
  `<org> requires a passing review to reach production.` is rendered from it; no name is compiled
  into the bundle.
- **`prod.clean-gate` is fail-closed.** `passed` is true only for a run that is terminal — `clean`,
  or `blocked` with every blocking finding covered by an unexpired approval — that reviewed the
  package preview runs and is that package's newest run, so a later run that blocked is never hidden
  behind an earlier pass — a patch run counting only while preview runs the image it deployed, the
  status read's `promote_run` test (*Patch runs*; amended 2026-10-18, #2086). The checks run in this order, and the first that fails is the `why`:
  no run (`no run has looked at this app`); `pending` or `running` (`<run> is <state>`); `failed`
  (`<run> ended failed: <reason>` — a failed build carries no blocking finding and must not read as
  clean); blocking findings left unapproved (`<run> ended <state>: <n> blocking finding(s), no
  approval`); nothing in preview (`nothing is running in preview`); another package in preview
  (`<run> reviewed <version>; preview runs <version>`); a newer run of that package
  (`<run> is not <version>'s newest run: <newer run> is`). **Amended 2026-10-01**
  (`canyonv2-5r11.11`, `.12`, decision D4) — a reason names versions, never runs. These strings
  replace every `<run>` reason above: `<version>'s review is <state>`; `<version>'s run failed:
  <reason in words>`; `<version>'s review found <n> blocking finding(s), not approved`; `Nothing in
  preview yet — canyon deploy puts it there`; `the review named is <version>'s; preview runs
  <version>`; `<version> has a newer review than the one named`; passing, `<version> passed review:
  no blocking finding` or `<version> passed review: every blocking finding approved`.
  **Every `<n>` in these reasons counts entries** (finding.md, *One problem, one entry*; amended
  2026-10-11, #2013), each with a finding no unexpired approval covers, so the go-live checks say the
  number the review's headline and the CLI say.
  **Production counts its own placements** (**amended 2026-10-04**, #1559): the blocking findings
  above are the ones that block before preview. After the newer-run check and before passing, one
  check more: a rule placed blocking before production whose row below fails — it counts findings that
  block there with no unexpired approval, the review ran before the rule did, its AI review was skipped
  or ran out of room, or `deps.known-vuln`'s built app (below) could not be checked or counts a
  package — fails `prod.clean-gate` too, with `why` `<version>'s review isn't clean for production. Fix
  its findings that block production, then deploy to preview again.` It never counts those findings
  itself: the rule's own row counts them, and one finding counted on two rows reads as two (**amended
  2026-11-08**, #2723: it said `<version>'s review found <n> blocking finding(s) before production, not
  approved` beside the rule's own count). So the
  receipt never reads `passed review` above a rule that fails before production: the rows never
  contradict each other.
  **A failure reason in words** (**amended 2026-10-04**, #1496) is what a person reads wherever a
  failed run's reason is shown: the receipt, the dashboard's runs table, the CLI's verdict, and the GitHub check.
  - `interrupted`: `it was cancelled, or another run of this app replaced it`
  - `error`: `Canyon itself failed`, then, when the run names the step it failed in (`failed_in`,
    amended 2026-10-04, #1645), ` while ` and the step's words: `scan` `scanning the package`, `review`
    `reviewing the package`, `resources` `making the app's resources`, `build` `building the package`,
    `deploy` `starting the app`. Those words are the receipt's `why`, the GitHub check's and the
    dashboard's runs table's. Where the verdict is shown whole — the CLI's, its ai format's, and the
    app page's — a run with `failed_in` adds the line `Run it again. If it fails again, an admin can
    look up run <run id> in Canyon's server log.`, and the server logs that run, its app, the step and
    the error's text (never a request body or a value) at the moment it ends the run, so the line is
    true. A run that ends `error` for a reason the owner or an admin acts on carries no `failed_in`
    and no such line — its last `build.log` line already says why: no front door set (D5's line), a
    release the org's platform refused, reported failed or removed, or never accepted or reported on
    (design/HAND-OFF.md §3) — nor does one whose own bookkeeping failed (reading the org's settings).
  - `model`: `the reviewer's model failed`
  - `build`: `the package did not build`
  - `detect`: `no buildpack on this host recognised the package`
  - `start`: `the app did not stay up`
  - `no_room`: `the cluster had no room to run the build`
  - `scan_no_room`: `the cluster had no room to scan the package` (**amended 2026-10-08**, finding.md,
    *Canyon-run tools*)
  - `image_gone`: `the image production ran is no longer on this Canyon` (**amended 2026-10-11**, #2055:
    a rollback review's, the gate-runs row)
  - `not_platform_only`: `more than Canyon's system libraries would have changed` (**amended 2026-10-18**,
    #2086: a patch run's, *Patch runs*; **amended 2026-11-01**, #2605: it said `run image`)
  - `not_same_inputs`: `the rebuild did not have the same code, build settings or dependencies` (**added 2026-11-02**, #2117: a
    rebuild patch's, *Patch runs*)
  - `patch_regressed`: `the patched image had a new vulnerability`
  - `patch_ineffective`: `the patch did not fix what it was for`
  - `reverted`: `the patched app exited or restarted, so Canyon put the image it replaced back`

  A reason this list doesn't name reads as itself. A run that failed isn't a review that failed.
  `build`, `detect`, `start`, `no_room` and the five patch reasons happen after the review passed; `scan_no_room` before it,
  so nothing was reviewed and there is nothing to fix or approve: the same deploy goes through once
  the cluster has room. A rule with
  nothing set to check against — `stack.blessed` while the snapshot carries no `approved_buildpacks` (none set), its `why`
  `no approved stacks set`, `prod.egress-allowed` while the org's platform runs preview and a promotion goes to it
  too (its row, below), and `owner.required` on a host with sign-in off while the app has no owner and the rule is at its default, Advisory, where
  no person signs in to own one (an org that turned it Blocking asked for an owner, and the block stands), its
  `why` `no owner needed: sign-in is off on this Canyon` (**amended 2026-11-15**, the tour's visitor run) — carries `unset: true`: it renders neutral (`·`, grey), never as a
  failure — whatever its state it is never in `blocked_by` — and stays on the receipt so an admin sees it. `<version>` is the package named the way
  *Naming a version* says — its label, else its id without the `pkg_` prefix — because the page
  renders these words verbatim and a person reading them says "v1.4", not "pkg_9e40": **a server
  reason never carries a `pkg_` id.**
  A blocking finding is one whose `canyon.blocks` the server derived against the run's snapshot
  (snapshot.md: the rule's state is `blocking` and its severity high or above), never what a
  producer's log claimed. The run row's counts never net an approval. An approval changes two answers: this receipt, and the
  next deploy carrying that finding, which goes ahead when every blocking finding is approved — its
  verdict row lists the approvals it went ahead under, and the run still ends `blocked`.
- `rules[]` is the four go-live rules — `prod.clean-gate`, `prod.human-approval`,
  `stack.blessed`, `owner.required` — **in the snapshot's order**, then `platform.known-vuln` and
  `prod.egress-allowed`, each while it is on
  (its state not `off`; below), then every other rule placed before
  production (snapshot.md, *Two gates*: it carries `production`) in the snapshot's order (design/EXPERIENCE.md
  D14). With none placed and those two off, it is exactly the four. Each is `{id, name, blocks, passed, why, unset?}`.
  `name` is the snapshot's name byte for byte. `blocks` is true iff the rule's snapshot state is
  `blocking`, or the rule is `prod.human-approval` and `prod_approval` is on (above). (The two `prod.*` rules; the other two are advisory and show a ✗ that blocks
  nothing — amended above: an amber `!`). `passed` is what the rule's producer saw; `why` is one factual line naming what it
  looked at — the run and its blocking count net of unexpired approvals, the actor's kind, the
  owner, the stack. **A placed rule's row** reads the app's effective snapshot (snapshot.md) when the
  receipt is read: `blocks` is true iff its `production` is `blocking`; it passes when no finding of it
  in the run the receipt is evaluated for blocks before production — derived as `canyon.blocks` is,
  from the `production` state — without an unexpired approval (an approval covers its finding in both
  gates), and `why` names the count, in entries as above: `<version>'s review found <n> blocking finding(s), not approved`,
  else `<version> passed review: no blocking finding` or `<version> passed review: every blocking
  finding approved`. **Fail closed:** a rule placed now that was not on in the snapshot the run
  journaled has no findings to read: it does not pass, and `why` is `<version> was reviewed before
  this check ran; check it again`. It reads the run only when that run is the one `prod.clean-gate`
  passes on — ended, of the package preview runs, and that package's newest run: else it does not
  pass and `why` is `prod.clean-gate`'s reason for the run (no run, under way, failed, another
  package, a newer run), never a count read from another version's review. A placed rule an agent
  reviews (snapshot.md: producer `llm-review`) whose agent's check on the run ended `skipped`
  (events.md, `check.completed`: the host had no model when the run ran, the model ran out of room
  for its answer, the source was over 16 MiB or held a file too large for one model call or one not valid UTF-8, or the model did not answer in time) has no findings to read either: it does not pass, and `why` is `<version>'s AI
  review did not run; check it again once a model is configured`, or, when the agent's run in the
  verdict carries finding.md's out-of-room note, `<version>'s AI review ran out of room for its answer;
  check it again` (**amended 2026-10-04**, #1500), or its over-16-MiB note, `<version>'s AI review did
  not run: the source is larger than it accepts (16 MiB)` (#1555), or its note of a file too large for one
  call, `<version>'s AI review could not read a file too large for one model call; the review names it`,
  or its note of a file not valid UTF-8, `<version>'s AI review could not read a file that is not valid
  UTF-8; the review names it` (amended 2026-10-16, #2280),
  or its out-of-time note, `<version>'s AI review did not answer in the time it allows; check it again`,
  or, on a verdict stored before the split with the note of the 2 MiB then, `<version>'s AI review did
  not run: the source was over the 2 MiB it took then; check it again, as a larger app is now reviewed in
  parts` (amended 2026-10-15, #2274). The sample is `placed_receipt` in
  `contracts/testdata/app-page/claims-portal.json`.
  **`deps.known-vuln`'s row reads the built app too** (design/PATCHING.md §3.6; decided 2026-11-01,
  #2355): what only the image holds — a build step's download, a buildpack outside Canyon's builder —
  is in no lockfile its review read. Once its review's row would pass, with `why` `<passed>` that row's,
  it reads the image the receipt is for (as `platform.known-vuln`'s row does, below) and counts what
  finding.md's *The built app* counts; the first that holds is its `why`:
  - the image could not be checked: `<version>'s built app could not be checked: <reason>`, and it does
    not pass. While `platform.known-vuln`'s row (below) reads the same image as not checked, whatever
    that rule's state, `<reason>` is that row's `why` after `could not check: ` (`no image scan
    recorded` as it is). Else it is the first of these to hold over the layers holding a package of
    class `app` or `unknown`, bottom first: a layer with no match, `the image has not been matched yet`;
    a match built after the read, `a stored scan of the image does not read`; a match over 5 days old,
    `its vulnerability database is over 5 days old`; a rescan of its app layers that did not match them
    all (finding.md, *The built app*: it marks this row alone), `its last rescan did not finish`; then a
    match naming a package its layer does not hold, `a match names a package the image does not hold`;
  - one package counted: `<name> <installed> is in <version>'s built app, but its review didn't see it:
    <fix>`, and it does not pass. `<fix>` is `<fixed> fixes it` (`fix it` when `<fixed>` names more than
    one version, `, ` between them) for its one advisory, `no fix is published for <id>` when that
    advisory names none, and `<n> advisories are published against it` past one;
  - more: `<n> known-vulnerable packages are in <version>'s built app, but its review didn't see
    them: <name> <installed> and <n - 1> more`, the first package the most severe, then by name and
    installed version, and it does not pass;
  - every package it would count approved (below): it passes as its review's row does, with `why`
    `<passed>; its built app's known-vulnerable packages its review didn't see are approved: <name>
    <installed>`, and ` and <n - 1> more` after that past one, the first in the order above, and
    `; Canyon couldn't check <k> package(s) in it` after that while `<k>` such packages were not checked;
  - else it passes as its review's row does, with `why` `<passed>; its built app holds no
    known-vulnerable package its review didn't see`, and `; Canyon couldn't check <k> package(s) in it`
    after that while `<k>` such packages were not checked.

  Each is cut to 200 code points. **A package of these is approved as a blocking finding is** (amended
  2026-11-02, #2610; it said no approval covers one): it is in no run's findings, so its approval names it by
  its fingerprints alone (finding.md, *The built app*), filed by the dependency's approve of the run the
  receipt is read for (the approve rows). It is not counted while approvals under `(project,
  deps.known-vuln, fingerprint)` cover every fingerprint of it for that run, as `runs.CoversBlocking`
  covers a blocking finding of it — never its author's, and at the deploy step only while each has 10
  minutes yet to stand (`runs.ShipMargin`); one fingerprint left uncovered counts it. Once the row read the
  built app and checked it, with any package counted or approved, it carries **`built_app`**: each package
  it counts, then each approved, in the order above, as `{name, version, advisories: [{id, fixed}],
  fingerprints, approved}`: `fixed` as trivy's `FixedVersion` wrote it, `""` for none published,
  `fingerprints` every one an approval of the package names, `approved` whether approvals cover it. No
  other row carries it. Placed advisory, the row blocks nothing. A rule placed before preview alone reads no image.
  `stack.blessed` reads the buildpacks that ran in the build of the
  image preview runs (executor.md, *The buildpacks that ran*; recorded on the deploys row) against the
  snapshot's `approved_buildpacks` (snapshot.md), by id — the ids the build reports, trusted as reported —
  judged in this order: none approved (the key absent) is unset, `why` `no approved stacks set`; a
  deploy whose recorded list is absent or empty — an executor from before, a deploy or a reused image
  built before this, or a group the executor could not read whole — does not pass, `why`
  `no stack recorded`; any buildpack not approved does not pass, `why` `not approved: <buildpacks>`, the
  ones not approved; else it passes, `why` `built with <buildpacks>`. `<buildpacks>` is each buildpack's
  recorded `name`, else its id, in the order they ran, joined by `, ` — advisory in the demo snapshot, it
  never blocks (amended 2026-10-04, #636: the approved list was `stacks`, whose languages and frameworks
  no build reports). `why` and `org` are
  plain text, never markup. The verdict and its reason are the server's; the client renders
  them and never paraphrases (D6: what passed sits next to what didn't).
- **`prod.egress-allowed`'s row** (design/EGRESS.md §3.4; **amended 2026-11-06**, #2125: it reads, where it
  could not check). `blocks` is true iff its state is `blocking`, but on a rollback (below); a promotion it refuses
  is `gate_not_clean` (the promotions row). It reads what preview connected to against what production allows now, when the receipt is read,
  never a run, and a promotion reads it on the transaction it decides in. **Two windows**, and **the margin, 90 s**
  (`egressSeenMargin`), hangs off `deployed_at` in both, never off a first go-live: a live tunnel's `last_seen_at` moves only at its periodic entry, every 60 s, and
  reaches its row at the flush after, within 10 s and that flush's 20 s of `POST /callers` at most, so a tunnel opened
  before go-live and connected after it is on a row a window holds. Stricter, never looser: what the outgoing version
  connected to in the margin counts too. **Rows** read from the earlier of the first go-live of the image preview
  runs, the oldest `deploy.completed` of its digest in preview, and `deployed_at` less the margin: deploying the same
  image again clears nothing it did past that first go-live, a new image does, and the margin hangs off the running
  deployment, so a call just before the first go-live, which may be the outgoing version's, counts only until a deploy
  goes past it. **Gaps and the records' keep** read from `deployed_at` less the margin, when the running deployment
  went live, since their lines say a redeploy clears them. A row in its window is one of preview's whose `last_seen_at` is at
  or past that time, whatever its last outcome, and a gap in its window one whose `to_at` is. A row is allowed as *The
  check* decides for `prod` today, by the check's own two steps: a name and port it refuses in every mode — an IP
  address, `invalid`, a port other than 80 and 443 — is not allowed, under `observe` too; then a platform destination
  is; then `observe` allows it; then an entry of `prod`'s allowlist must, and a mode that is neither allows nothing.
  `prod`'s deploy row is not read: a promotion carries preview's spec. These run in this order, and the first that
  holds is its `why`; the row does not pass:
  - preview's deploy row's `run_by`, or the org's `run_by` setting (the preferences row), reads as neither `canyon` nor `org`, or doesn't read: `could not check: Canyon can't read preview's connections right now. Try again in a few minutes. If this persists, ask an admin.`;
  - **both say `org`** (amended 2026-11-07, #2125; design/EGRESS.md §3.4): the org's platform runs preview and a promotion goes to it too, so no allowlist of Canyon's meets the version. Both are read with preview's row, and this alone is no failure: `unset: true`, `why` `not checked: preview runs on your own platform`, neutral whatever the rule's state and never in `blocked_by`;
  - preview's row says `org` and the setting `canyon`: a switch back leaves preview's row as it was (design/HAND-OFF.md §1, *Switching*), and the promotion starts the image on Canyon, behind production's allowlist, with nothing read of what it connects to: `could not check: Canyon doesn't have a full record of preview's connections. Deploy to preview again. If this persists, ask an admin.`;
  - preview's row records no `canyon` spec, on Docker the app's `floor` is not `enforced`, so its calls may go around
    Envoy, or `run_config_at` is absent or later than `deployed_at`: a recreation moved preview onto that spec after
    it went live, and a deploy writes both with one `now()`. What it reached before is on no row, and no row is no evidence.
    Its line gives the fix the app's Outbound card gives (**amended 2026-11-08**, #2723: it said to deploy to preview
    again where a deploy can't help, beside a card that said otherwise): the app's `floor` `null`, so the host's via is not
    `canyon` and a deploy goes around Canyon again, `could not check: Outbound is Off on this Canyon, so it records none of this app's calls. An admin can turn it on in Settings › Outbound.`, naming the setting, never the network: under Off a null floor is an open network or one Canyon can't tell, a recreation under way or failed (**amended 2026-11-09**, #2777);
    on Docker, a `floor` of `not_enforced`, the app on its old network, which a deploy routed via `canyon` is refused on
    until it is recreated, `could not check: preview may skip Canyon until an admin recreates its network in Settings › Outbound.`;
    else `could not check: Canyon doesn't have a full record of preview's connections. Deploy to preview again. If this persists, ask an admin.`;
  - no `deploy.completed` of the image reads: `could not check: Canyon doesn't have a full record of preview's connections. Deploy to preview again. If this persists, ask an admin.`;
  - a row in the window is not allowed: `production does not allow <destinations>`, each once, sorted, joined by
    `, `: a name, a port other than 80 and 443 after it (`api.example.com:8443`), and `an invalid address` for the
    `invalid` row. `why` holds at most 200 characters, so past them it reads `<destinations> and <n> more`, and
    `<n> destinations` (`1 destination`) when not one name fits;
  - the running deployment's window opens more than 30 days before the read, past the rows' and the gaps' keep (design/EGRESS.md §2.4):
    `could not check: Canyon doesn't have a full record of preview's connections. Deploy to preview again. If this persists, ask an admin.`;
  - the receiver's heartbeat (`egress_receiver`) is absent, or more than a flush and a half from the read, either
    way, as the server's clock stamps it and the database's reads it: older, what a boot would write as
    `receiver_down`, a span no gap can say yet: `could not check: Canyon can't read preview's connections right now. Try again in a few minutes. If this persists, ask an admin.`;
  - a gap in the window fails closed (`egressGapHolds`, the one place that decides it): preview's own `over_cap`,
    `could not check: preview connected to over 500 destinations, more than Canyon tracks. Connect to fewer and deploy to preview. If this persists, ask an admin.`;
    one that names no app, `could not check: Canyon doesn't have a full record of preview's connections. Deploy to preview again. If this persists, ask an admin.`
    Another app's `over_cap`, and production's, hold nothing.

  With nothing read at all, as preview leaving between two reads, it does not pass: `could not check: Canyon can't read preview's connections right now. Try again in a few minutes. If this persists, ask an admin.`
  None holding, it passes: `production allows everything <version> has connected to so far`. **On a
  rollback review's receipt** it reads no preview, and a person rolling back is never held by what an unrelated
  version connects to, so its `blocks` is false whatever its state, and it passes: `not compared: a rollback puts back what production ran`.
  With `prod` under an allowlist that refuses a destination a person dismissed there (design/EGRESS.md §2.5), it
  does not pass, an amber `!` that stops nothing, and lists them as `production does not allow` does: `this version may need <destinations>, which production does not allow. A person can change that on the Outbound card.`.
  The dashboard's and the CLI's rollback say that line before the person promotes. On a production whose deploy row
  says `org` (amended 2026-11-07, #2125) it lists none and passes on its first line: the Outbound card has no `Allow`
  there, so the line would send a person nowhere. A row that doesn't read, or says neither value, leaves the line as it
  was, since it stops nothing.
  **To a responder with no grant** (the status row) outbound is technical detail, so a line naming destinations
  reads `production does not allow everything preview connected to. The app's team can fix this.`, and a rollback's the first of its two.
- **`platform.known-vuln`'s row** (design/PATCHING.md §3.3). It is the go-live rule that judges an image
  and never a run. It reads the image the receipt is for: preview's deploy row's `image_digest`, or, on a
  rollback review's receipt, the image that run ships. `blocks` is true iff its state is `blocking`. These
  checks run in this order, and the first that holds is its `why`; the row does not pass:
