| internal: `POST /egress/dropped {calls}` | **what Envoy lost on its way to the receiver, as egress-control read it** (design/EGRESS.md §2.4; **added 2026-11-06**, #2374): not under `/api/v1`, no session, on the policy's listener and authenticated as `GET /egress/policy` is, above, else `401`, no body; it reads and changes no app, so it names no level of design/PERMISSIONS.md · the body `egress.schema.json`'s `dropped_report`, exactly `{calls}`, at most 1 KiB: a whole number from 1 to 2⁴⁰, the entries Envoy's `access_logs.grpc_access_log.logs_dropped` rose by, a worker's 16 KiB buffer full and the stream taking no more; or `null`, entries were lost and no one counted them: Envoy started again (`server.uptime` or a counter went backwards — a decrease is never a negative gap, and what the new Envoy counted is sent as well), a stream to the receiver failed to open (`cluster.receiver.upstream_rq_pending_failure_eject` rose: the entries written to it are gone, and Envoy counts them as flushed, never as dropped), the first read of an egress-control found either counter above zero, which one before it may have reported in part, or the read failed, past 2 s among the ways: an unread count is never none · two failed reads report nothing: no socket file in egress-control's first minute, before Envoy's first answer, is no Envoy yet, which lost nothing, and past that minute is a read that failed, and a read egress-control's own stop cut short says nothing of Envoy, its last read deciding · egress-control reads Envoy's stats on the admin socket every 10 s, first 10 s after it starts, and once more as it stops, each read 2 s at most and its reports 5 s of their own · `400 {error:"invalid_report"}` for any other body, a key spelt otherwise, a key twice or anything after it among them · `503 {error:"no_receiver"}` with no receiver, and once its last flush has begun: egress-control keeps what it has not delivered, summed, and sends it again at its next read · `204`: taken, and the next flush writes it, as it writes the receiver's own `dropped`, over that flush's span: one `envoy_dropped` gap, and one beside it with `calls` `null` for what no one counted · each replica reports its own Envoy, and a flush sums them |
| internal: `GET /egress/route-value?app=<app>&env=<env>&destination=<destination>` | **the one route that answers a route's value** (design/EGRESS.md §4.1; **added 2026-11-07**, #2135; served from #2137 on): not under `/api/v1`, no session, on the policy's listener and authenticated as `GET /egress/policy` is, above, else `401`, no body; it serves egress-control and no person, so it names no level of design/PERMISSIONS.md · `200` `egress.schema.json`'s `route_value`, `{rev, value}`, with `Cache-Control: no-store` · `404 {error:"no_route"}` when the policy names no such app env or the env holds no route of that destination · `400 {error:"invalid_request"}` for any other query, a key twice or a key it does not name among them · no log line, error or audit row carries the value; one log line names who asked, the app, the env and the destination |
| internal: `GET /egress/leaf?destination=<destination>` | **one inject destination's leaf, for SDS** (design/EGRESS.md §4.2, §4.5; **added 2026-11-08**, #2704; #2139 serves it to Envoy): not under `/api/v1`, no session, on the policy's listener and authenticated as `GET /egress/policy` is, above, else `401`, no body; it serves egress-control and no person, so it names no level of design/PERMISSIONS.md · `200` `egress.schema.json`'s `leaf`, `{root, cert, key, chain, not_after}`, with `Cache-Control: no-store`: a leaf minted for this answer and kept nowhere — one SAN, the destination, its own ECDSA P-256 key, valid from 5 minutes before it was minted to 24 hours after, or to the intermediate's end when sooner, signed by the intermediate; `chain` the intermediate, then, under an admin's sub-CA, the sub-CA and the certificates above it but the self-signed root; `root` the signing root the chain ends at, the policy's `signing_root` · **the intermediate** is the server's, minted under the signing root and held in its memory alone, its key never written anywhere: valid from 5 minutes before it was minted to 7 days after, or to the signing root's end when sooner; a CA that signs leaves alone, a path length of 0; its name constraints critical, permitting every destination an app env holds a route to and excluding every IPv4 and IPv6 address; minted again when the signing root or that set of destinations changes, when the server starts, and before a leaf would end after it · `400 {error:"invalid_request"}` for any query but `destination` once · `404 {error:"no_route"}` unless an app env holds a route to `destination`, as stored · `503 {error:"no_signing_root"}` while no root is live: no leaf, and the policy names no route, so nothing is forwarded without the header · no log line, error or audit row carries a key; one log line names who asked and the destination |

### A change waits for the gateway

**Every write above that changes the egress policy** — `PUT /api/v1/egress` (via, mode, caps), an env's mode, an allowlist entry's
`PUT` and `DELETE`, a route's `PUT` and `DELETE` and `from-secret` — **answers only once every live egress-control
replica holds the version it made** (design/EGRESS.md §3.2, Convergence; **added 2026-11-12**, #2789, the owner's
decision): after its transaction commits, and never under it, the server reads the policy's `seq` and waits, at most
30 s, until no replica seen in the last 30 s holds another epoch's version, none, or a lower `seq` — the wait a
deploy's `/run` makes before it starts (the deploy row) — so a call the change refuses is refused, by every replica,
from its answer on. With no live replica none is behind. A refused write writes nothing and waits for nothing; a taken one that changes nothing may still wait, for a change made before it.
**Past the wait the change stands**, never rolled back: the answer, its status and body as ever, carries
`Canyon-Egress-Pending: <replica>`, the first replica behind by name, or `Canyon-Egress-Pending: unread` when the
wait could not be read — the database's error or the request's context — so it never says every replica holds what
it could not see. An answer without the header is one every live replica took. Settings › Outbound, the app's
Outbound card and the needs list say so in place of the change's confirmation (design/EXPERIENCE.md D5).

### The check

egress-control (`canyon-server egress-control`, the sidecar beside Envoy, design/EGRESS.md §3.1) answers
Envoy's ext_authz, gRPC `envoy.service.auth.v3.Authorization/Check` over localhost, from the policy it holds
(`GET /egress/policy`, above). Envoy asks with `failure_mode_allow: false` and a 5 s timeout (**amended 2026-10-19**, #2119: was 1 s, which a new pod's first call, waiting on the refetch below, could outlive): no answer is
`503` with the body `Canyon's egress check did not answer, so this call was not made.`

- **What it reads** of a CheckRequest: the direct peer's address, whether the method is `CONNECT`, the
  scheme, the authority and, on Kubernetes, the `Proxy-Authorization` (a pod's token, below) — nothing else, on an
  inner request what *Interception* names aside. It **never logs a
  CheckRequest**, whole or in part: an absolute-form request's path and query are in it (design/EGRESS.md §2.4).
- **The caller** is the policy's for the peer's address. None: a refetch (`caller=`, above), single-flight per
  address, asked again every 250 ms while the policy names no caller for it — an ask the server answers without
  one, or does not answer — for 4 s at most, inside Envoy's 5 s, so a slow or empty refetch is `unknown_caller`,
  never a `503` (**amended 2026-10-19**, #2119: a new pod calls out before its address reaches the cluster's API,
  about a second, and a cold server-to-executor path has room; a known caller's call waits on none), then, still
  none, `unknown_caller`. An address the server's answers to a refetch named no caller for — a `200` or a `304`, never
  a `503 caller_unresolved` or no answer — is `unknown_caller` at once, with no ask, for 15 s, the server's confirm interval, and at most 64 refetches run at once on a replica, a check past them
  `unknown_caller` at once (**amended 2026-10-19**, #2119): an address no one holds costs at most one refetch, 16
  asks, per 15 s; a caller whose app env the policy does not name is `unknown_caller` too.
  Callers expire, unlike envs and allowlists (**amended 2026-10-18**, #2113): once the policy egress-control holds
  is more than 30 s old it names no caller, so the refetch decides, and while the server cannot answer it every
  call is `unknown_caller`, never one under an address another app's pod may hold now. **Static stability is
  therefore 30 s** (**amended 2026-10-19**, #2116): past 30 s without the server every call is refused
  `unknown_caller` — fail closed, the bound #2113 chose. egress-control keeps nothing across a start: it starts
  empty, asks with `have` empty, and is ready only while it holds a policy no more than 30 s old, which only a
  `200` gives it (design/EGRESS.md §3.1, readiness: `canyon-server egress-control probe` exits 0 then alone;
  **amended 2026-10-19**, #2119: a replica cut off from the server leaves the gateway's Service, so the others serve); a policy kept on disk could make no call
  succeed, its callers past the bound, and would only be a path to tamper with.
- **On Kubernetes, a pod's token** (**added 2026-11-09**, #2142; design/EGRESS.md §4.3; contracts/executor.md, The pod's
  token). The check also reads the call's `Proxy-Authorization`.
  - **Its runtime is its own setting**, never the policy's (#2142's review): egress-control is on Kubernetes when
    its policy URL is `https://` and it holds `CANYON_EGRESS_POLICY_TOKEN_FILE`. There a policy with no
    `kubernetes`, or of `format` `1`, is refused whole, and the one held serves on; on Docker a policy carrying
    `kubernetes` is refused whole. A policy is never read as the other runtime's, so an older server's policy,
    a rollback of the server alone or a gateway upgraded first, never makes a Kubernetes egress-control name a
    caller by its address and intercept its call.
  - **A call that carries one** is the app env whose `account` a valid projected token names, from any address,
    whether or not the env is `token_only`, and no one else. The token is verified offline against the policy's
    `kubernetes` and must hold all of these:
    - signed by one of its `keys`, RS256 or ES256;
    - the issuer its `issuer`;
    - audience `canyon-egress`;
    - within its `nbf` and `exp`;
    - `sub` `system:serviceaccount:canyon-apps:<account>`, its `kubernetes.io` namespace and ServiceAccount the
      same, and bound to a pod.
  - **Never longer than an address's caller.** A token names its caller only while the policy is at most 30 s old
    and its key set at most 10 minutes.
  - **A token that fails** any of these is `unknown_caller`, never its address's caller.
  - **A call that carries none** is its address's caller, as above, but never an env the policy marks `token_only`:
    its pods are named by their token alone (no downgrade).
  - **Every OK** to a call that carried a `Proxy-Authorization` removes it, so the token never moves past the check.
  - **A call named by its pod's address alone** is never intercepted (*Interception*): during a rollout the
    earlier pod still passes by its address, the new one by its token, and only the token's call is injected.
  - On Docker no token names a caller.
- **What it takes** (**amended 2026-10-19**, #2116): a policy whose `format` is one it knows — `1`, and from
  #2142 `2` — checked against the fields it knows, strictly, and every field it does not know ignored, so a server release may add
  one without taking egress down; a `format` higher than it knows, a policy failing the fields it knows, one
  naming an address or an app env twice, one carrying a key twice or a key that differs from a field it knows
  only by case (`Callers`, which a case-blind decoder would read as `callers`), or one whose `epoch` is the one
  held and whose `seq` is lower is refused, whole, and the one held serves on. An answer to an ask made before the policy held was installed
  is dropped, whatever its version: asks never race a newer policy back out.
- **The destination** is the `CONNECT` authority, else the absolute-form URL's, its port the URL's or the
  scheme's (`http` 80, `https` 443), **canonicalised** (design/EGRESS.md §3.3) by one function, whose
  entry form the routes use: lower-case; one trailing dot dropped; IDNA (UTS 46, its lookup profile)
  to A-labels; userinfo refused; every IP-literal encoding refused — dotted, integer, hex, octal, bracketed,
  v4-mapped: a bracketed or IPv6 host, and a name whose last label is a number, decimal or `0x` and hex
  digits, which a client reads as an IPv4 address; single-label names refused; labels ≤63, names ≤253;
  ports 80 and 443 only. An allowlist entry `*.stripe.com` allows any name any depth below `stripe.com`,
  never `stripe.com`. **The canonical name must be the host as the app sent it**, lower-cased in ASCII with
  one trailing dot dropped, else `invalid_destination`: UTS 46 maps or drops some characters — a
  zero-width space, fullwidth letters, `。`, `①` — and Envoy dials the host it was given, so the check never
  allows a name other than the one Envoy dials. An entry a person types is mapped (`bücher.de` is
  `xn--bcher-kva.de`); a host on the wire never is. An inner request's `:authority` (HTTP/2) or `Host`
  (HTTP/1.1) must equal the destination (layer 3).
- **The decision**, in order, in every mode: `unknown_caller`; a destination the canonicalisation refuses,
  `invalid_destination`, or `port` when it refuses the port alone; past the policy's `caps.new_names` new names an
  app env a minute, 30 until an admin sets it (design/EGRESS.md §3.3; **amended 2026-11-09**, #2691), across every replica — never
  that many for each — `rate_limited`: each replica lets through its share, the cap over `CANYON_EGRESS_REPLICAS`, the most replicas that run at once, rounded down (one
  on Docker; on Kubernetes three, two and a rollout's surge, so 10 each): stricter than 30 an app env, never
  looser, as an app's calls spread over the replicas unevenly, for static stability — the share asks nothing
  of the server, as a count kept there would for every new name, so a replica cut off from it still holds the
  bound; egress-control does not start with `CANYON_EGRESS_REPLICAS` unset or outside 1 to 30; a name is
  **new** to an app env unless it is known: on the env's `known[]` (the names a call of it went through to in the
  last 30 days, above), allowed by an entry of its allowlist, a platform destination, or one this replica let through
  for it since it started (it keeps 500 an app env); a name refused is never let through, so it counts nothing;
  counted per minute of the clock. **The bound, stated**: at most `caps.new_names` new names an app env a minute — a cap below the replicas leaves
  each a share of none, so no new name gets through, which egress-control says once — while at
  most `CANYON_EGRESS_REPLICAS` replicas run and none is replaced within the minute; a replica replaced within
  the minute adds at most its share once more, as its successor starts from `known[]`, which holds what was
  recorded, not what the minute let through before it was (**amended 2026-10-19**, #2116); a platform
  destination, allowed — a front-door host the canonicalisation refuses, an IP address or a single label such
  as an in-cluster `keycloak`, is none: a call to it is `invalid_destination` first, and such a provider is
  reached directly, `CANYON_KUBE_SIGNIN_DIRECT`, never through Envoy (#2341); `observe`, allowed; `allowlist`, allowed when an entry of the env's allowlist
  allows the destination, else `not_allowed`; a mode that is neither, `not_allowed` — never `observe` (fail closed).
- **`not_allowed` is refused** as every other reason is, now the platform destinations carry the endpoints the
  front door's discovery names (the front-door row), so `allowlist` never refuses an app's own sign-in
  (**amended 2026-10-19**, #2344; from #2116 until then it was recorded, its request filed, and let through).
- **A refusal** is `403`, or the status its row below names, the header `Canyon-Egress: <reason>`,
  `content-type: text/plain; charset=utf-8` and one line, its body below; nothing goes upstream and nothing is
  resolved, `upstream_failed` aside: Envoy tried to resolve and reach the destination, and the request never left.
  The RBAC filter's `403` to a request that already carries `x-canyon-intercept` (*Interception*) is Envoy's own,
  with no `Canyon-Egress` header. A refusal is no SARIF finding.
- **One log line per denial**: the app, env, destination as a record
  keeps it (the receiver row, above), reason and Envoy's stream id (at most 64 bytes, else none) — never a path,
  a query or a header.

| `Canyon-Egress` | Body |
|---|---|
| `not_allowed` | `Canyon refused this call: <destination> is not on <app> <env>'s outbound allowlist. A person on the app's team can allow it in Canyon: <url>` — `<destination>` canonical, `<env>` `preview` or `prod`, `<url>` the policy's `public_url` and `/#/apps/<app>/outbound` |
| `unknown_caller` | `Canyon refused this call: Canyon does not know which app is calling.` |
| `invalid_destination` | `Canyon refused this call: an app may call a host name on port 80 or 443, never an IP address.` — never echoing the authority, which may carry userinfo |
| `port` | `Canyon refused this call: an app may call out on port 80 or 443 only.` |
| `rate_limited` | `Canyon refused this call: <app> <env> called more than <n> new destinations this minute. It can call them from the next minute, or an admin can raise the limit in Settings › Outbound.` — `<n>` the policy's `caps.new_names` (**amended 2026-11-09**, #2691) |
| `inject_requires_tls` | `Canyon refused this call: use https:// for <destination>. Canyon adds the app's credential only over https.` |
| `inject_unavailable` | `Canyon could not add the app's credential for <destination>, so it did not send this call. Try again. If this persists, ask an admin.` — status `502` |
| `misdirected` | `Canyon refused this call: this connection goes to <destination>, but the request is for another host.` — status `421` |
| `upstream_failed` | `Canyon could not connect to <destination> or verify its certificate, so it did not send this call.` — status `502`, Envoy's own answer, only on `UF` or `DF` and only for a destination with a route (*Interception*) |

- **Dynamic metadata** in namespace `envoy.filters.http.ext_authz`, on every answer, which the call's
  access-log entry carries to the server's receiver (the receiver row, above; **amended 2026-10-19**, #2116):
  `app` and `env`, the caller's (none for `unknown_caller`), and `outcome`, `allowed` or `refused`, with
  `reason`, the `Canyon-Egress` reason, on a refusal. A record's
  `last_outcome` is its `outcome`; an entry that carries none is read as the receiver row says (Envoy's `UAEX` or `NR` is `refused`).
- **Egress requests** (design/EGRESS.md §3.4; **amended 2026-10-19**, #2116), filed by the receiver from the
  records' `reason`, in its flush's transaction. The first `not_allowed` of `(app, env, destination)` the
  records carry files one, `pending`, actor `canyon`, one audit row `egress.request` `detail{env, destination}`;
  each later one counts on it (`refusals`, `last_refused_at`), dismissed or not. `refusals` counts the `not_allowed`
  calls alone, the ones an allowlist entry would let through; the destination's record row counts every call to
  it on its port, whatever its outcome and reason, so a call refused otherwise — `unknown_caller` while the policy
  does not yet name a new pod's address, `rate_limited`, `port`, a check that did not answer — is a call of the row
  and no refusal of the request (**amended 2026-11-08**, #2718: #2708's run read one refusal beside two refused
  calls, and each flush adds what it took to both). A destination whose request is
  dismissed files no new one; an allowlist entry that allows it closes it, and files none while it allows it —
  a call from before it was allowed reaching the server after — and a refusal after that entry is removed
  files a new one. At most the admin's `caps.pending` pending an app env, **50** until one is set (**amended 2026-11-09**, #2691): past them a refusal files none, and its row still
  records it.

### Interception

Layer 3 (**added 2026-11-07**, #2135; design/EGRESS.md §4.2; built by #2140 and #2141). A route changes what
happens to a call *The decision* allowed, and nothing else. A destination with no route goes on as above: Envoy
decrypts nothing of it.

- **Trust.** Canyon intercepts an env's routes only while the host's `credential_routes` is `true` (the egress setting
  row, above; **added 2026-11-09**, #2143) and the root signing the current leaves (*the signing root*,
  design/EGRESS.md §4.5) is among the env's trusted roots: the roots every instance of the env that may be serving
  trusts. Each `/run` — a deploy, a promotion, a rollback, a platform patch, a network recreation — carries the roots
  of its `egress.ca`, by SHA-256 fingerprint, or none while `credential_routes` is `false` or when its `env` names one of `SSL_CERT_DIR`,
  `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` and `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`, and the deploy row records the trusted roots, `egress_roots`: before
  the `/run`, the old set intersected with the carried one, in the transaction that names the env, so it moves `seq`
  and the deploy's wait for every replica covers it; the carried set alone only once the executor answers that the
  new version is up and the previous instance is gone — on Docker its `200`, on Kubernetes a `200` whose
  `previous_gone` is `true` (contracts/executor.md). A failed, interrupted or stuck `/run`, or a retry that records
  nothing, leaves the intersection; nothing recorded, or a row that cannot be read, is the empty set: fail closed.
  The policy names an env's routes only while the signing root is among them (the policy row); otherwise each
  destination passes through untouched, as one with no route, so no TLS breaks, and the read gives each route's
  `waiting`. The two refusals below take the app env's advisory lock, as the receiver's flush does. The orderings:
  - a secret, then a route: the route's write is `409 trust_secret`;
  - a route, then a secret: the secrets `PUT` is `409 route_trust`;
  - two such writes at once, a route's and a secret's: each takes the app env's advisory lock before its check, so
    the second sees the first and is refused;
  - a secret deleted, then a route added while the running version still carries its own CA: that version's `/run`
    carried no root, so the route waits, `redeploy`, until a `/run` without it has replaced every instance;
  - a version that predates #2143: its row records no roots, so its routes wait for a deploy;
  - a promotion, a rollback, a platform patch, a network recreation: each is a `/run`, recorded as above;
  - a rollout's overlap, old and new instances serving at once: the intersection holds until the old are gone;
  - a failed Docker start, a Kubernetes rollout that never completes, or one whose previous pods outlive the bound,
    `previous_gone` `false`: the intersection stays;
  - a retry that starts nothing: it records nothing, so the intersection stays;
  - a root's rotation, or a switch to a BYO sub-CA: leaves stay signed by the old root until no env holding a route
    lacks the new one, and an env still lacking it at the old root's end — its certificate's, or 60 days after the
    rotation began, the sooner — waits, `redeploy`: fail closed. The new root is among the roots each `/run` carries from
    the rotation on, and the old one until its end (`POST /api/v1/egress/ca/rotate`; **amended 2026-11-08**, #2704);
  - an env holding a route counts only while Canyon runs it, its deploy row's `run_by` `canyon` (**added 2026-11-08**,
    #2704): one with no deploy row, or one the org runs, has no instance Canyon intercepts, so it
    holds no root back; it trusts nothing, the empty set, so its routes wait, `redeploy`, until a `/run` records the
    roots it carried, the signing root among them;
  - a root passing its end changes the signing root with no row written: the policy's next answer moves `seq` first (the
    policy row), so egress-control drops the routes of an env that lacks the new signing root and fetches its leaves again;
  - via to `off`, or `run_by` to `org`: refused while any route exists, `409 credentials_exist`;
  - a from-secret whose route would wait: `409 would_wait`, so a moved key is never stranded; a `PUT` may wait.

- **`x-canyon-intercept`**, the header that sends a `CONNECT` to the one internal listener, value `1`. Only
  ext_authz #1 sets it, on a `CONNECT` to a destination with a route; an RBAC filter, the first on `:3128`, answers
  `403` to any request that already carries it, before any check, and every other OK names it in
  `headers_to_remove`, with `clear_route_cache: true`.
- **`caller`**, the dynamic metadata ext_authz #1 adds to that OK in namespace `envoy.filters.http.ext_authz`,
  `<app>/<env>`. `set_filter_state` copies it to the filter-state object **`canyon.caller`**, with
  `shared_with_upstream: ONCE`, so each tunnel carries its own.
- **`destination`**, the dynamic metadata ext_authz #1 adds to that OK beside `caller` (**added 2026-11-08**, #2140;
  asked by its security review): the canonical name it approved the tunnel for. `set_filter_state` copies it to the
  filter-state object **`canyon.destination`**, with `shared_with_upstream: ONCE`, as `caller`, so a tunnel approved
  for one destination never carries a request for another. ext_authz #1 adds it, alone, to the OK of the absolute-form
  inject branch too (**amended 2026-11-08**, #2141): an OK that carries it is a call to a destination with a route, by
  which Envoy's `local_reply_config` tells one (*Upstream*, below).
- **`x-canyon-caller`**, the header the internal listener's `header_mutation` overwrites from `canyon.caller`, or
  empty without one (`keep_empty_value: true`, so an empty value still overwrites; **amended 2026-11-08**, #2140's
  review: it said `-`, which the pinned Envoy never sends, and a mutation of an empty value it would skip, leaving the
  app's own header), whatever the app sent. ext_authz #2 reads it and removes it, so the upstream never sees it.
- **`x-canyon-destination`**, the header the internal listener's `header_mutation` overwrites from
  `canyon.destination`, or empty without one, as `x-canyon-caller`, whatever the app sent (**added 2026-11-08**,
  #2140). ext_authz #2 reads it and removes it, so the upstream never sees it.
- **`context_extensions`**, set per filter chain, carry one key, `destination`.
- **ext_authz #1, a destination with a route**, once the decision allowed the call: a `CONNECT`, the OK above, and
  Envoy ends the app's TLS with a certificate naming that destination alone, so a client speaking no TLS finds no
  filter chain and its connection closes; an absolute-form `https://` request, no interception, the value read and
  the header overwritten as ext_authz #2 does, on this answer; a plain `http://` request, `inject_requires_tls`.
- **ext_authz #2, each inner request**, reads `x-canyon-caller`, `x-canyon-destination`, `context_extensions`'
  `destination` and the `:authority` (HTTP/2) or `Host` (HTTP/1.1), in order: a caller empty — what an inner hop that
  skipped ext_authz #1 carries — or one the policy does not name, `unknown_caller`; an `x-canyon-destination` other
  than `context_extensions`' `destination` — a tunnel ext_authz #1 approved for another destination, or for none,
  empty — `misdirected`, its body naming the destination the
  tunnel was approved for when it names one (**added 2026-11-08**, #2140); an authority other than the destination,
  both compared canonically — lower-case, the one trailing dot dropped by the chain's connection manager before the
  check (`strip_trailing_host_dot`, which its routes match after too), a `:443` dropped, so `Host: api.stripe.com:443` is
  the destination — `misdirected`; *The decision* made again of the destination for the caller, past the caller, the
  canonicalisation and the rate limit, by the same function — a platform destination, the mode, the allowlist, as
  they stand now — its reason (`not_allowed`): a route outlives the allowlist entry that let its destination
  through, so a tunnel opened before the entry went refuses its next request (**added 2026-11-08**, #2140); a route of
  that caller and destination gone, or a value it cannot read, `inject_unavailable`. Else OK: the route's header
  overwritten whatever the app sent, by `scheme` (`bearer`, `Bearer <value>`; `basic`, `Basic` and the value in
  base64; `raw`, the value), and `x-canyon-caller` and `x-canyon-destination` removed. Its answers carry the dynamic
  metadata *The check* names, and a refusal its one log line.
- **The value.** egress-control asks for a route's value on first use (`GET /egress/route-value`, above), one ask
  a route at a time, each 2 s at most and none past 4.5 s from when its check began, a refetch of the caller
  included, inside Envoy's 5 s: past it the call is `inject_unavailable`, never the `503`. It holds a value in memory
  alone, never on disk and never in a log line, uses it only under the `rev` the policy names for the route, and
  drops it when that `rev` changes, the route goes or the `epoch` does; as a `rev` never repeats, a value is never
  kept for a route deleted and set again. When ext_authz #1 or #2 cannot tell whether a destination has a route, or cannot read its
  value, the call is `inject_unavailable`: never forwarded without the header.
- **Upstream**, Envoy originates TLS and verifies the destination's certificate, never resuming a session
  (`max_session_keys: 0`). The intercepted route and the absolute-form inject branch retry nothing past a connect
  failure — `retry_on` `connect-failure` and `reset-before-request` alone — so a request that reached the destination
  is never sent twice. Envoy's `local_reply_config` gives the `upstream_failed` body only to a `UF` or `DF` reply
  (a connection, handshake, certificate or name that fails before the request is sent) for a destination with a
  route — on `:3128`, a request that is no `CONNECT` whose check's OK carried `destination`, its `metadata_filter`,
  and on the internal listener every request, there to a `UH` reply too, as its dynamic forward proxy's sub-clusters
  answer a name that does not resolve no healthy upstream (**amended 2026-11-08**, #2141); a plain `CONNECT` that fails never reads
  a line about credentials, and an intercepted `CONNECT` that finds no internal listener stays `503` (design/EGRESS.md
  §6). A failure once the request has gone is
  Envoy's answer with no `Canyon-Egress` header and no body of Canyon's, as the destination may have taken it.
- **A record.** Each inner request is one call of the row of its destination and port: `last_protocol`
  `intercepted`, attributed by `canyon.caller`, as its source is no app's address, its path dropped on receipt as
  every entry's. The tunnel's own entry counts as any tunnel's, `connect`. Each chain logs its inner requests to the
  receiver under the log name `intercept`, with the receiver's token and `canyon.caller` alone of the tunnel's state;
  the destination is the authority, canonicalised, as every entry's, so a tunnel and its requests share the row of the
  destination on 443, and the last of them to end sets its `last_protocol` (**amended 2026-11-08**, #2740).
  A row's `last_credential_refusal` is its latest call refused while adding a credential, `{reason, at}`, `at` when
  the receiver took its entry: `reason` the check's `inject_requires_tls`, `inject_unavailable` or `misdirected`;
  `misdirected` too for an inner request the internal listener answered `421` itself, which asks no check; and
  `upstream_failed` for an inner request Envoy flagged `UF`, `DF` or `UH`. **A request on `:3128` that is no
  `CONNECT` never records `upstream_failed`**: whether its destination has a route is the policy's, which the
  receiver does not read, so only an intercepted call's is recorded. An earlier refusal never replaces a later one,
  and the row's other fields are its last call's whatever its reason (**added 2026-11-09**, #2692).

## Access

The app's team (*App teams*, above). Every route here but `POST …/access-requests` is its owners' and
admins' (`403 owner_required`); none is routed on a host with auth off — nothing is shared where
nobody signs in, so the `/api/v1/` catch-all answers `404 {error:"not_found"}`. **The access read** is
one shape, answered by the GET and by every PUT: `{everyone, entries[], roles[], requests[]}` —
`everyone` is `{roles[]}` when the app is shared with everyone, else `null` · `entries[]` its `email`
and `group` grants, oldest first, each `{kind, grantee, display, level, roles[], granted_by, granted_at}`:
`display` the person's name as their grant holds it, else the address, and the group's name for a
group; `granted_by` the actor triple · `roles[]` every app role a package of the project declares
(`[roles]`, the upload row), `{name, description}`, by name, the newest package's line for each ·
`requests[]` the pending access requests, oldest first, `{email, display, requested_at, level}` — `level` the asker's when they asked, `none` or `viewer`, a read-only member asking to join the team; `null` on a request from before Canyon recorded it, never `none` (**added 2026-11-09**, #2774).

| Route | Request | Response |
|---|---|---|
| `GET /api/v1/projects/:id/access` | — | `200` the access read |
| `PUT /api/v1/projects/:id/access/:kind/:grantee` | `{level, roles[]}` — `roles` omitted is `[]` | `200` the access read — **share**: writes the grant whole, replacing one the grantee already holds · `:kind` ∈ `email, group, everyone`, `:grantee` an address for `email` (stored lower-cased), a group name for `group`, and `everyone` for `everyone`, else `400 {error:"invalid_grantee"}` · `level` ∈ `owner, contributor, viewer`, and only `viewer` for `everyone`, else `400 {error:"invalid_level"}` · a role no package of the project declares is `400 {error:"unknown_role", message}`, naming it · any other body is `400 {error:"invalid_body"}` · a `group` grant while `groups_claim` is unset (Preferences) is `409 {error:"groups_unsupported", message:"This Canyon doesn't read groups from your sign-in yet. An admin sets the groups claim in Settings."}` · a `group` grant the app does not hold, or one the PUT widens — a higher level, or a role the grant does not hold — is `409 licence_required` (*Licence*), `feature` `group_sync`, while no valid or grace licence names it; a PUT that repeats or narrows a group grant (a level no higher, roles it already holds), and `DELETE`, never are, so a licence that lapses changes nobody's access and never stands between an owner and taking access away (**added 2026-11-14**, #2879; **amended 2026-11-14**, #2881's review) · lowering the app's last grant at `owner` is the same `409 {error:"last_owner"}` as unsharing it · an `email` grant deletes that address's access request in the same transaction · one audit row, `share app`, `detail{kind, grantee, level: {before, after}, roles: {before, after}}` — `grantee` the word `everyone` for everyone — each `before` `null` for a new grant — adding an owner is this row with `level.after` `owner`; a PUT that changes nothing writes none |
| `DELETE /api/v1/projects/:id/access/:kind/:grantee` | — | `204` — **unshare** · `404 {error:"not_found"}` when there is no such grant · deleting the app's last grant at `owner` is `409 {error:"last_owner", message:"add a successor first"}`, and nothing changes · one audit row, `unshare app`, `detail{kind, grantee, level, roles}`, what the grant held, `grantee` the word `everyone` for everyone |
| `POST /api/v1/projects/:id/access-requests` | — | `201` — **ask for access**, the no-access screen's button and a read-only member's: a member at level `none` or `viewer` asks with their session's address and display, one request per address — asking again while a request is pending is `201` and changes nothing, writing no audit row, unless the asker's level changed since (`none` to `viewer` through a new grant, or back), which updates the request's `level` and writes one `request access` row (**amended 2026-11-12**, #2774), but another person asking from that address replaces the pending request's subject, the one a share binds the grant to, with a fresh `request access` row — only from an address the provider asserted verified: another person unverified over a pending request that names a subject is `409 {error:"request_pending"}` and changes nothing; a viewer asks to change the app, which its owners grant by sharing it at `contributor` (amended 2026-10-05, #1706: a read-only member had no way to ask) · the request records the asker's level when they asked, `none` or `viewer`, resolved by the server through their own grant, their groups' and the org's (`everyone`), never by the dashboard; a level it can't resolve is `500 {error:"internal"}`, logged, and records nothing (**added 2026-11-09**, #2774) · `409 {error:"already_on_team"}` for a caller at `contributor` or above, and for an admin · one audit row, `request access`, `detail{email}` · no email is sent |
| `DELETE /api/v1/projects/:id/access-requests/:email` | — | `204` — **dismiss** · `404 {error:"not_found"}` when that address has no request · one audit row, `dismiss access request`, `detail{email}` |

## Sessions

| Route | Response |
|---|---|
| `DELETE /api/v1/sessions?subject=` | `204` — without `subject`, signs the caller out by revoking their own session. With `subject`, revokes every session whose actor subject **or email** matches, and is `403 {error:"admin_required"}` for a member. A `subject` matching no session is still `204`. One audit row either way: the act is recorded, not the count. |
| `GET /api/v1/device-grants/:user_code` | `200 {requested_by, requested_at, expires_at, approver: {display, email}}` for a pending grant, `approver` being the caller: the person whose access approving hands over, named from their session so the page can say who is approving, dashes and case ignored and the Crockford look-alikes I, L and O read as 1 and 0 · `404 {error:"not_found", message}` when no pending grant has that code, when it has expired, and when `CANYON_AUTH` is off (the family is not routed). Any signed-in member may read, approve and deny any pending code **from a browser session**: a CLI session (`flow` `device`) is `403 {error:"dashboard_required", message:"open the link canyon login printed in your browser, signed in to Canyon, to approve or deny a CLI sign-in"}` on all three routes, before the grant is read, with one `denied` audit row (amended 2026-10-12: a CLI session approving a code mints itself a fresh one, so a stolen CLI token could renew itself for as long as its person stays a member; design/AUTH.md §4.2). The flow is cross-device by design, the screen's defence is the sentence *Only approve a code you generated yourself*, and guessing a code only donates the guesser's own identity, so these routes are unbounded. |
| `POST /api/v1/device-grants/:user_code/approve` | `204`: the grant is bound to the caller's session, and the CLI's next poll is answered with that person's identity, copied from the session row · `404` as above; a code already approved or denied is gone. No audit row: the poll's `sign in` row is the record. |
| `POST /api/v1/device-grants/:user_code/deny` | `204`: the grant is deleted and the next poll is `400 {error:"access_denied"}` · `404` as above. No audit row; one log line. |

## Database sessions

A person reads or changes an app's Canyon-provisioned Postgres **as themselves** (design/DATABASE-SESSIONS.md;
**added 2026-10-18**, #2106): `canyon db query` runs one statement through the server, and `canyon db
connect` relays a local client's Postgres connections over the person's sign-in. A **database session**
(`dbs_` + 16 hex) is one person × one app database × one mode, `read` or `write`, opened and ended by
Canyon; the server logs in as the person's own role, never the app's (contracts/provisioner.md, *People*).
Who may: design/PERMISSIONS.md, *Database sessions*, which wins on conflict. Admins pass the level
checks; `db_prod`, the reason, the browser rule and `held` bind them as anyone. The shapes — the read,
the bodies, the refusals, the four audit actions and the relay's wire — are `db-session.schema.json` and
`db-query.schema.json`.

| Route | Minimum | Answers and refusals |
|---|---|---|
| `POST /api/v1/projects/:id/db/query` `{env, mode, sql, resource?, reason?, limit?, timeout?, replace?}` | `contributor`; prod write `owner` | `200 {db_session, columns: [{name, type}], rows, row_count, truncated, command, rows_affected?, duration_ms}` · `400 invalid_sql` (empty, over 64 KiB, or refused by the database before it runs (more than one statement, a syntax error, a table or column that isn't there), with the database's message: the statement is Parsed, where Postgres also analyses it, before it is Executed, and a refusal at Parse is this, never a match on the message's words; **amended 2026-11-09**, #2118) · `400 invalid_limit` (outside 1–10,000) · `400 invalid_timeout` (outside 1 s–5 min) · `422 statement_timeout` · `422 query_failed {sqlstate, message}`, with sqlstate `54000` also when a row is over the 10 MiB answer cap (**amended 2026-11-12**, #2118) · `503 {error:"check_failed", message:"Canyon couldn't check this database session just now, so nothing ran. The session is still open: try again."}` when a check can't be judged (Canyon can't read what it checks): nothing runs and nothing closes, a database session closing only on a check that answers a reason (**added 2026-11-14**, #2865) · `429 {error:"busy", message}` past a cap on the database connections this server makes for people, judged before the login, so a refused query runs nothing and its database session stays as it was: `message` exactly `You already have 10 database connections open: close one and try again.` at the person's 10 or `Canyon is at its limit of <n> database connections: try again in a minute.` at the host's, `<n>` `CANYON_DB_MAX_CONNECTIONS` (design/DATABASE-SESSIONS.md §6; **amended 2026-11-15**, #2814: a relay and a query each hold one while its connection is open) · in `read` mode, `409 read_only_unsafe` with the connect's `message`, its items the second check's alone (`EXECUTE on dblink function <schema>.<name>(<args>)`, `EXECUTE on SECURITY DEFINER function <schema>.<name>(<args>)`, `USAGE on foreign-data wrapper <name>`), the definer item for any `SECURITY DEFINER` function outside the system schemas the person's role may run, whoever owns it and whatever it calls, so an app that wants read queries revokes `EXECUTE` from `PUBLIC` on its definers (**amended 2026-11-15**, #2920's review), and `503 check_failed` with the message above when the check can't read what it judges in a database that answered, never `502`, judged inside the login before the statement runs, so nothing runs; a `READ ONLY` transaction holds its own connection, never a second one `dblink` or `postgres_fdw` opens (contracts/provisioner.md, `NoSecondConnection`; **added 2026-11-15**, #2918) · and the open's refusals |
| `POST /api/v1/projects/:id/db-sessions` `{env, mode, resource?, reason?, replace?}` | as above | `201` new · `200` attached — the database session read, **never a credential**. In order: `404` app · `403 team_required` · `403 person_required` · `409 db_sessions_off` (**added 2026-11-12**, #2835) · `400 invalid_env, invalid_mode` · `409 db_prod_off, db_prod_read_only` · `400 reason_required` (amended 2026-11-12: a host whose production is off says so before asking why) · `403 owner_required` · `403 dashboard_required` (only an open of a new prod write) · `409 held` · `409 no_database, which_database {resources[]}, database_not_ready, driver_unsupported, session_open {mode}` · `429 busy`, judged before the login so a refused open grants nothing · `409 unsafe_schema, limits_unsupported, not_as_made, database_not_ready` and `502 database_unreachable`, which only the login finds; `unsafe_schema`'s `message` exactly `PUBLIC can still create in schema public, which only its owner or a superuser can revoke. Ask an admin of this app's database to run REVOKE CREATE ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC, then try again.` (**amended 2026-11-14**, #2826: a person reading it doesn't know "the apps database"); `not_as_made`'s `message` exactly `Canyon didn't log you in to the database of <app> in <env>: <found>. Ask an admin of this app's database to remove that, then try again.`, `<found>` one of `your role <role> is also a member of <roles>, by a grant Canyon can't revoke` · `your role <role> has an attribute, or is granted to <roles>, which Canyon never gave it`, or, granted to none, `your role <role> has an attribute Canyon never gave it` · `the group role <role> has a login, an attribute or a membership Canyon never gave it` · `the database sets the role of every session that connects to it (ALTER DATABASE … SET role)`, `<role>` as Postgres names it and `<roles>` joined by `, ` (contracts/provisioner.md, `*NotAsMade`); a database the login finds gone is `database_not_ready` with the message above; `502 database_unreachable` is a database that didn't answer, and only that (**added 2026-11-14**, #2910: a refusal the login found read as one to try again) |
| `GET /api/v1/projects/:id/db-sessions` | `contributor` | `200 {db_sessions[]}`, open, oldest first · `403 team_required` |
| `GET /api/v1/db-sessions/:sid/connect?startup=` | its person | WebSocket `canyon-db.1`; `startup` base64url JSON of the client's parameters, ≤ 4 KiB · `403 cross_origin` for any request carrying `Origin` (the CLI sends none; a browser always does) · `400 invalid_startup` when `startup` is not base64url JSON of at most 4 KiB, holds over 64 names, a name outside `^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_.]{0,62}$` or starting `_pq_.`, a value over 1,024 characters, holding a NUL or not UTF-8, `user`, `database` or `replication` in any case, `application_name` in any case but lower, or two names the same but for case, as Postgres reads a name in any case and Canyon sets `application_name` itself (**amended 2026-11-12**, #2129), refused before anything is dialled (**added 2026-11-11**, #2129) · in a read session the backend starts with `default_transaction_read_only=on` over any the client sent, which the client can `SET` off: a default, not a guard; the read group's grants are what enforce read (design/DATABASE-SESSIONS.md §4, *Known limits*; **amended 2026-11-12**, #2129) · in a read session, `409 {error:"read_only_unsafe", message:"Canyon didn't open a read connection: anyone connecting could still change data here (<found>). Ask an admin of this app's database to revoke those rights, then connect again."}` before anything is dialled while the person's role could change data by any path (contracts/provisioner.md, `ReadOnly`): `<found>` at most five rights, sorted, joined by `, `, then `, and <n> more` past five, each one of `<PRIV> on <schema>.<relation>`, `MAINTAIN on <schema>.<view>`, `<PRIV> on sequence <schema>.<name>`, `CREATE on schema <name>`, `CREATE on database <name>`, `EXECUTE on SECURITY DEFINER function <schema>.<name>(<args>)`, `UPDATE on large object <oid>` (**added 2026-11-14**, #2826), and, from its second check, `EXECUTE on dblink function <schema>.<name>(<args>)`, `EXECUTE on SECURITY DEFINER function <schema>.<name>(<args>)` for any definer outside the system schemas, whoever owns it, and `USAGE on foreign-data wrapper <name>`: a role that can open a second connection from inside the database, which neither its grants nor a read-only transaction hold (contracts/provisioner.md, `NoSecondConnection`; **added 2026-11-15**, #2918); either check that can't read what it judges, in a database that answered, is `503 check_failed` with the message below, never `502` · `409 unsafe_schema` with the open's `message`, when `public` was opened to `CREATE` since, never `502` (**added 2026-11-14**, #2826) · `409 not_as_made` and `409 database_not_ready` with the open's `message`, never `502` (**added 2026-11-14**, #2910) · `404` not theirs, an owner's or an admin's included · `409 db_sessions_off` (**added 2026-11-12**, #2835) · `409 limits_unsupported` · `410 {error:"session_ended", reason}` · `429 {error:"busy", message}` past a cap on the database connections this server makes for people, judged before the login, so a refused one dials nothing: `message` exactly `You already have 10 database connections open: close one and try again.` at the person's 10 or `Canyon is at its limit of <n> database connections: try again in a minute.` at the host's, `<n>` `CANYON_DB_MAX_CONNECTIONS` (design/DATABASE-SESSIONS.md §6; **amended 2026-11-15**, #2814: a relay and a query each hold one while its connection is open) · `502 database_unreachable` when the database doesn't answer the login, and only then · `503 {error:"check_failed", message:"Canyon couldn't check this database session just now, so it didn't connect. The session is still open: try again."}` when a check can't be judged, before anything is dialled: nothing closes (**added 2026-11-14**, #2865) · close code `4000`, reason a `close_reason` or `canyon_restarting`: `expired` the moment the database session ends and `signed_out` the moment the sign-in that connected ends, each on a timer of the relay's own, the 15-second checks closing it for every other reason (**amended 2026-11-12**, #2129) · close code `1011` when Canyon can't check the database session, which ends that one connection and not the database session: the client may connect again, and the next connect runs every check (**added 2026-11-11**, #2129), and when Canyon can't write the connection's start to the client · close code `1001` when the client answers no ping within 20 seconds · each binary message from the client at most 1 MiB, which `canyon db connect` frames its bytes to; a bigger one closes `1009` (**amended 2026-11-12**, #2129) |
| `GET /api/v1/db-sessions/:sid` | its person | `200` the database session read, open or closed, and once closed `closed_at` and `close_reason`: how `canyon db connect` learns, with no client connected, that its database session ended and why · `404` for anyone else, an owner or an admin included (**added 2026-11-12**, #2129) |
| `POST /api/v1/db-sessions/:sid/cancel` `{process_id}` | its person | `204`, cancelled or not · `404` for anyone else, an owner or an admin included |
| `DELETE /api/v1/db-sessions/:sid?if_idle=` | its person · its app's owners · admins | `204` · `409 in_use` with `if_idle=true` and a live connection · `404` for anyone else |

**The database session read**: `{id, app, env, resource, mode, person: {subject, display, email}, role,
reason?, opened_at, expires_at, idle_timeout, last_activity_at, connections, queries, bytes_from_db,
bytes_to_db, relayed_by?, yours}` — `person.email` the address the sign-in stores, `""` on a host with sign-in
off, whose anonymous admin (`subject` `anonymous`) has none, `role` the login it runs as, `reason` its first, on every prod one,
`idle_timeout` whole seconds (1800 in preview, 900 in prod), `expires_at` at most 8 hours after
`opened_at` in preview and 1 hour in prod, and never past the sign-in it rides on, `relayed_by` the
server (its `durable_executors` id) relaying a live `connect`, `connections` the connections opened in it so far, a query's
and each `connect` client's, open or closed, never how many are open now, and `yours` whether it is the reader's
own. The query's `db_session` is this read whole.

- **The body.** `env` ∈ `preview, prod` (else `invalid_env`), `mode` ∈ `read, write` (else
  `invalid_mode`). `reason` is 1–500 characters, at least one visible, with no control, format (Unicode
  `Cf`) or bidirectional character. A prod request needs one only when it would open a database session
  or be the first attach to one of a sign-in other than the opener's — the requests that write a row,
  which carry it: there, missing, empty or otherwise is `reason_required`, its message saying so. Every
  other query or attach — from the sign-in that opened it, or one that has attached before — needs
  none, and one it sends is not recorded: the database session's reason covers it. `resource` names
  one of the app's postgres resources in that env, as the app's current package (its newest upload)
  declares them — the same for both envs, so a hold that takes production down never hides its database: one the app does not declare is `no_database`,
  none named when it declares two is `which_database {resources[]}`, and a declared one whose
  `resources` row is missing, not `ready`, or has no driver configured is `database_not_ready`. `limit`
  is 1–10,000 rows, 500 when absent; `timeout` a duration as Go's `time.ParseDuration` reads it (`30s`,
  `1.5s`, `5m0s`), 1 s to 5 min, 30 s when absent.
- **`db_prod`** is judged as the preferences read answers it, never as stored: `off` while sign-in is
  off, whatever the row holds — by every open, attach and check.
- **`db_sessions`** (the preferences row) turns database sessions as a whole on or off, `off` by default.
  While it is `off`, every open, attach, query and connect is `409 {error:"db_sessions_off", message:"Database sessions are off on this Canyon. An admin turns them on in Settings › Databases."}`,
  judged after `team_required` and `person_required` and before anything is issued; production is off
  with it, whatever `db_prod` says. Turning it `off` closes every open database session `turned_off` and
  ends its relays: this replica's at once, every other's at its next pass (**added 2026-11-12**, #2835).
- **Attach, don't replace.** One open database session per person × app × env × resource. An open or a
  query in the same mode attaches to it (`200`); the other mode is `409 session_open {mode}` unless
  `replace` is true, which closes it `replaced` and opens the new one in one transaction; its message is `you have a
  <mode> session to <app> <env> open: run it again with --replace to switch.` A query without `replace` switches
  the other mode's database session on its own while no client is connected through it — the driver's `Activity`
  counts none of the person's backends — and is `session_open` while one is, or while the count can't be read
  (amended 2026-11-12, #2843's review: a query never drops a live `connect`). A `read` query
  may run on an open `write` database session, in a `READ ONLY` transaction. **`dashboard_required` is
  judged only when the request would open a new prod write**: an attach from any of the person's own
  sign-ins — the CLI joining the prod write its owner opened in the dashboard — passes it, and meets
  `held` and every other check. Its body is `{error:"dashboard_required", message:"open it in Canyon's
  dashboard"}`.
- **The login.** An open takes the person role's lock and judges `session_open` before any grant, so a
  refused open grants nothing. Every login sets the role's group membership to the row's mode and the
  role's limits (design/DATABASE-SESSIONS.md §6) first, so the role never holds rights its row does not
  name; a database that refuses a limit is `409 limits_unsupported`, on the open and on `…/connect`.
  Once the login has connected, still under the lock, the role is set `NOLOGIN`, so a password a person
  sets on their own role opens nothing. Its TLS is never weaker than the app's own connections
  (contracts/provisioner.md, *People*).
- **The query** runs one statement: `BEGIN READ ONLY` in `read` mode, at most `limit` rows, cut at a
  row boundary at the limit or 10 MiB with `truncated` true. `rows` are Postgres's text output, `NULL`
  as `null`; a column's `type` is Postgres's `format_type` (`bigint`, `character varying(80)`);
  `rows_affected` only in `write` mode. A statement is never logged or stored; `422 statement_timeout`
  is never retried by the CLI, since a write may have run.
- **The checks**, every 15 s, on every connect and on every query, close a database session with its
  `close_reason`: `signed_out` · `access_changed` · `setting_changed` (`db_prod` no longer allows it, or
  `prod_approval` turned on while a prod write opened from a non-browser sign-in is open) ·
  `database_gone` · `expired` · `held` (a prod write's production held) · `idle` (30 min in preview, 15
  in prod) · `turned_off` (`db_sessions` turned off, #2835); and `closed` (its person), `ended` (an owner or admin), `replaced`. A check
  that can't be judged closes nothing on a connect or a query; the sweep closes on it once it fails four sweeps in a
  row, with that check's reason (**added 2026-11-14**, #2865). **Each connection** — a
  relay, a query — records the sign-in it came on, and the check judges that sign-in and its expiry too:
  one revoked, expired or no longer a member's ends that connection, close code `4000`, reason
  `signed_out`, its backend terminated, while the database session goes on under its opener. A connect
  to a closed database session answers `410 {error:"session_ended", reason}`.
- **The trail** (*Audit categories*): `db session opened` and `db write session opened`,
  `detail{db_session, env, resource, mode, role, reason?, expires_at, via_tool}`; `db session attached`,
  once per database session and sign-in, the first time a sign-in other than the opener's attaches —
  the opener's is on the trail by the open row — `detail{db_session, env, resource, mode, reason?,
  via_tool}`, `reason` that request's; `db session closed`, `detail{db_session, person, reason,
  duration_s, connections, queries, rows_returned, bytes_from_db, bytes_to_db}`, `reason` the `close_reason`. `via_tool` is `query` on the
  query route, `dashboard` on an open or attach from a `browser` sign-in, `connect` otherwise. The actor
  is who acted: the person; the owner or admin who ended it; `{kind: "canyon", subject: "db", display:
  "Canyon"}` for a check. Each `403` here writes one `denied` row; every other refusal writes none and
  one log line. A query adds to the counters and writes only the open row or the first attach row
  it makes.

## Telemetry

**Added 2026-11-12** (#2807). A Canyon server sends one report of counts a day to Canyon's collector, on by
default (**amended 2026-11-12**, the owner: as Grafana and Coder do, no admin has to see it first)
(`telemetryEndpoint`, cmd/canyon-server/telemetry.go): the shape is contracts/schema/telemetry.schema.json,
whose fields TELEMETRY.md lists, generated from it. Who may: design/PERMISSIONS.md, *Telemetry*, which wins
on conflict.

| Route | Who | Answers |
|---|---|---|
| `GET /api/v1/telemetry` | admin | `200` the schema's `state`: `{enabled, off_because?, report, last_sent_at, last_report}`, `report` the body the next send POSTs and `last_report` what the last send the collector answered carried, null before one, each a JSON string holding those exact bytes: a reader shows the string as it is, and never parses and re-writes it, since what is sent is the promise (**amended 2026-11-12**, #2807's web review). · `403 admin_required` |
| `PUT /api/v1/telemetry` `{enabled}` | admin; `enabled: true` in a browser | `200` the `state` after it. `enabled: true` lifts an admin's off and an `unreachable` one; it cannot lift `env`, the server's environment. It is a widening, a person's from the dashboard: `403 person_required` to an `agent` or `canyon` actor, and on a host with sign-in `403 {error:"dashboard_required", message:"change it in Canyon's dashboard"}` from any session but a browser's; `enabled: false` from any session (**amended 2026-11-12**, #2807). · `400 bad_request` for any other body · `403 admin_required` |

Sending: at most one POST a day across replicas, the replica that claims the day's row sends, 2 seconds,
no retry, no log line either way. A report's counts cover the period since the previous claim, at most a week, and say
how long in `period_hours` (**added 2026-11-15**, #2953): a rolling 24 hours sent every 23 to 24 lost a missed day and
counted overlaps twice. Off, and nothing sent, while any of these holds (`off_because`, the first
that does): `env` — `CANYON_TELEMETRY=0`, `DO_NOT_TRACK=1` or a non-empty `CI` on the server; `admin`;
`unreachable` — the first send could not reach the collector. The first send is the first day's, whether or
not anyone has read the report. Each `403` writes one `denied` audit row; a `PUT` writes one audit row, `PUT
/api/v1/telemetry` with `detail{enabled}`.

## Licence

**Added 2026-11-14** (#2879; the owner's decision: Canyon is AGPL-3.0 core plus a commercial Enterprise edition). A
licence unlocks Canyon Enterprise's features on one Canyon; the shapes are contracts/schema/licence.schema.json. Who
may: design/PERMISSIONS.md, *Licence*, which wins on conflict.

**The file** is `{payload, signature}`: `payload` the standard base64 of the payload's JSON bytes, `{customer,
issued, expires, seats, features[]}`, and `signature` the standard base64 of Canyon's Ed25519 signature over those
exact bytes. The server verifies it offline against the public keys compiled into it, a list so a key can be
rotated, and makes no network call. It reads the licence from `CANYON_LICENSE`, the file's text or, when that does
not begin with `{`, the path of a file holding it, read again at each use; else from the one row an admin's `PUT`
stores. `CANYON_LICENSE` wins.

**The state**, computed at each use from the server's clock: `none`, no licence; `invalid`, a licence that is not a
licence file, or a path the server can't read (`problem` `unreadable`), or one no compiled key verifies (`unsigned`);
`valid` before `expires`; `grace` from `expires` until `expires` plus 30 days (`grace_until`); `lapsed` after. Only
`valid` and `grace` unlock anything, and only the features the payload names that the server gates
(`allows`): `outbound_keys` (credentials Canyon adds to apps' calls: the egress setting's `credential_routes` and
the route rows), `platform_patching` (the preferences' `patching` and `rebuilds`) and `group_sync` (the preferences'
`groups_claim` and `group` grants). A name the server does not know unlocks nothing and is still shown. `seats` is
shown, never enforced.

**Fail closed, both ways.** Without a licence that unlocks a feature, adding it or turning it on or up is refused
`409 {error:"licence_required", message, feature}`, before anything is written, on the routes above that name it:
a credential for a destination the env holds none for, `credential_routes` turned on, `patching` or `rebuilds` turned
to `ask`, a groups claim set to another claim, a group grant added or widened. Rotating, narrowing, turning off and
removing never are: replacing a credential's value, a group grant at a level no higher with roles it already holds,
any feature turned off, anything removed (**amended 2026-11-14**, #2881's review: a paywall never stands
between an admin and a security fix). Nothing else reads the licence: what is set keeps working whatever its state — a
credential set keeps being added to calls, `patching` `ask` keeps patching, a group grant keeps granting — so a
licence that lapses never weakens the review, never stops what runs, and changes nobody's access. These promises are
Canyon Enterprise's: a server built without it is below. The `message`, by
`feature` (design/EXPERIENCE.md, D5):

- `outbound_keys`: `Adding credentials to apps' calls needs Canyon Enterprise. What's set keeps working; to change it, an admin adds a licence in Settings › Licence.`
- `platform_patching`: `Turning on patching needs Canyon Enterprise. What's set keeps working; to change it, an admin adds a licence in Settings › Licence.`
- `group_sync`: `Access by sign-in group needs Canyon Enterprise. What's set keeps working; to change it, an admin adds a licence in Settings › Licence.`

**Patching at upgrade** (**added 2026-11-14**, #2881's review). What an install runs when it upgrades keeps
running: each org's `patching` is stored explicitly (0114 wrote every row's), so a host that patched before keeps
patching whatever its licence, as a credential and a group grant set before keep working, on a server built with
Canyon Enterprise (one built without it patches nothing, below; amended 2026-11-15, #2923). A new install starts off:
at each start, while the host is in setup (`onboarded_at` null) and has no app, a server with no licence in force
naming `platform_patching` writes `patching` `off`, with one audit row, `patching off at start`, by `{kind: "canyon",
subject: "licence", display: "Canyon"}`, `detail{patching: {before: "ask", after: "off"}}`, so an admin reads why a new
host's patching is off (**amended 2026-11-14**, #2881's review); a start that finds it already `off` writes
none. One with such a licence leaves it `ask`. While the server's
`CANYON_PATCHING` is `on`, `patching` is `off` and no licence in force names `platform_patching`, Canyon patches
nothing: the server logs `canyon-server: CANYON_PATCHING is on, but Canyon patches nothing: patching is off in Settings › App hosting, and turning it on needs a licence, which an admin adds in Settings › Licence` once at start (**amended 2026-11-15**, #2938), and an admin's fleet read carries the
`patching_unlicensed` need.

**A server built without Canyon Enterprise** (**added 2026-11-14**, the owner's decision, #2913). Canyon Enterprise's
code is `ee/`, under the Elastic License 2.0; the released server is built with it, and `go build -tags noee` with
`ee/` deleted builds a server without it (design/ARCHITECTURE.md §8). The cut: the code that creates or changes an
Enterprise feature's configuration is Canyon Enterprise's; reading, enforcing and removing what is set are the core's.
A server built without it has no licence: `GET` answers `{state: "none", allows: []}`, a `PUT` is `409
enterprise_absent`, `CANYON_LICENSE` unlocks nothing (the server logs so once at start) and the `licence` need never
shows. Each change this section refuses for want of a licence it refuses the same, `409 licence_required` with the
same `message` and `feature`; removing, narrowing and turning off are never refused. What each feature does on such a
server beyond that is said where its code moves to `ee/`.

- **Platform patching** (`platform_patching`, `ee/patching`; **added 2026-11-15**, #2923): what decides and schedules
  Canyon's patches is Canyon Enterprise's — the tick's rebase and rebuild plans and their waves, filing patch approvals,
  the fleet approval's decisions, and turning `patching` or `rebuilds` to `ask`. Turning either to `ask` is `409
  licence_required`, `feature` `platform_patching`, after the preferences row's `403`s, `signin_required` and
  `patching_off`; turning either `off` is never refused, and patching at upgrade writes `off` as above. Whatever
  `patching` stores, such a server patches nothing: it reads `patching` as `off` wherever it decides whether it
  patches, so no patch run is planned, one queued ends at step 1 and one past it stops before its `/run` (*Patch
  runs*), no patch approval is filed and an open one is `outdated` (*Production approvals*), and none is decided for
  the fleet, while `GET /api/v1/preferences` answers what is stored. A patch already past its `/run` when such a server
  starts is watched to its end, reverted on an exit or a restart and its outcome recorded, as on any server; a watch
  that passes files no patch approval. A fleet approval and a resume of an identity are
  `409 licence_required`, `feature` `platform_patching`, after every other refusal of their rows, nothing written.
  While its `CANYON_PATCHING` is `on` it logs `canyon-server: CANYON_PATCHING is on, but this Canyon is built without
  Canyon Enterprise, so it patches nothing` once at start, in place of the line above, and an admin's fleet read
  carries the `patching_unlicensed` need whatever `patching` stores. The rescan, the image reads, the patch states and
  the identities read are taken as on any server.
- **Access by sign-in group** (`group_sync`, `ee/groupsync`; **added 2026-11-15**, #2924): such a server adds no
  `group` grant and widens none, and sets no groups claim other than the stored one, each `409 licence_required`,
  `feature` `group_sync`, after `groups_unsupported`. A sign-in still reads a person's groups by the claim set, and a
  group grant set before keeps granting its level. Repeating or narrowing a group grant, removing it and clearing the
  claim are taken as on any server.
- **Outbound keys** (`outbound_keys`, `ee/outboundkeys`; **added 2026-11-15**, #2922): Canyon
  Enterprise holds the code that writes a credential and the code that adds one to a call: the interception root and its
  leaves, the policy's routes, a route's value, and a `/run`'s `egress.ca` and placeholder variables. Without it: adding
  a credential, replacing a credential's value and moving a secret into one (`PUT` and `POST …/from-secret` on
  `/api/v1/projects/:id/egress/:env/routes/:destination`) are `409 licence_required` with `feature` `outbound_keys`,
  after every refusal before the write's (the level, the actor, the session, the destination, the body, the secret,
  `egress_external`); turning `credential_routes` on is the same `409`; `POST /api/v1/egress/ca/rotate` is the same
  `409` after its `403`s, its body unread; the admin's `ca` is `{signing: null, roots: [], rotates_at: null}`; the
  policy's `signing_root` is `null` and every env's `routes` `[]`, so no credential is added to any call, and a `/run`
  carries no `egress.ca` and no placeholder; `GET /egress/route-value` and `GET /egress/leaf` are `404 no_route`; and an
  egress-control built without it serves no interception listener and refuses a call the policy routes
  `inject_unavailable`, never sending it without its credential. Removing a credential and turning `credential_routes`
  off stay free, and a credential a server with it set is still listed, its `waiting` `{reason: "host"}`. A server with
  Canyon Enterprise answers as the rest of this contract says whatever its licence: replacing a credential's value is
  never refused for the licence there (#2881).

| Route | Who | Answers |
|---|---|---|
| `GET /api/v1/licence` | any signed-in member | `200` the schema's `state`: to a member `{state, allows}`; to an admin also `source` (`env` or `dashboard`, absent with no licence), and for a licence that verifies `customer`, `seats`, `issued`, `expires`, `grace_until` and `features`, for an invalid one `problem`; on a server built without Canyon Enterprise `{state: "none", allows: []}` to everyone |
| `PUT /api/v1/licence` `{licence}` | admin, a person, in a browser | `200` the admin's `state` after it. `licence` the file's text, at most 8 KiB. `403 admin_required`; `403 {error:"person_required", message:"A person sets Canyon's licence, in the dashboard: Settings › Licence."}` to an `agent` or `canyon` actor (**amended 2026-11-14**, #2881's review); on a host with sign-in `403 {error:"dashboard_required", message:"change it in Canyon's dashboard"}` from any session but a browser's · `409 {error:"set_by_operator", message:"CANYON_LICENSE sets this server's licence, so it can't be changed in the dashboard."}` while `CANYON_LICENSE` is set · `400 {error:"invalid_licence", message:"This isn't a Canyon licence, or Canyon didn't sign it."}` for one that is not a licence file or that no compiled key verifies, nothing stored · `400 bad_request` for any other body · on a server built without Canyon Enterprise `409 {error:"enterprise_absent", message:"This Canyon is built without Canyon Enterprise, so it can't use a licence."}` after the `403`s, nothing read or stored (**added 2026-11-14**, #2913). A licence that verifies is stored whatever its dates, replacing the one before |

Each `403` writes one `denied` audit row; a `PUT` that stores a licence writes one audit row, `PUT /api/v1/licence`,
`detail{customer, expires, features}`. The usage report (*Telemetry*) carries nothing of the licence.

## Platform

| Route | Response |
|---|---|
| `GET /api/v1/fleet?tile=&owner=&stage=&running=&stack=&q=&needs_you=&access=&scope=&held=&sort=&page=&cursor=&limit=` | `{tiles, stats, apps[], total, page, next, banner, viewer, unchecked, org_needs}` — the fleet page polls this · **`org_needs`** (**added 2026-11-09**, #2725) the org's own needs (*What needs a person*), which no app's row carries: to an admin each chat channel failing (`notify_failing`, *Integrations*), by `since` then `channel_id`, then the licence while it is in grace, lapsed or invalid (`licence`, *Licence*; **added 2026-11-14**, #2879), then patching the server is set to do that no licence unlocks (`patching_unlicensed`, *Licence*; **amended 2026-11-14**, #2881's review); `[]` to everyone else; counted into `needs_you`, so the rail and the browser tab say it · **`unchecked`** (**added 2026-11-09**, #2724) how many environments Canyon runs, of the apps the reader reads whole, this read could not tell are running (their `state` `unknown`: the executor down, slow or refusing), every page's: a `down` need can hide among them, so the screen says it couldn't check them and never says nothing needs the reader while it is above 0 · **`held=true`** — the fleet's Held filter (*The hold*): the apps whose production is held, among the rows whose `hold` the caller reads (contributor and above, every row for a responder or an admin), so it reveals nothing a row does not; only the exact `true` turns it on · a responder's row is the project read's as a responder reads it, `hold?` included · `viewer` is `{display, role, github_logins, github_link}` **here — a display name, a role, and no address** — the signed-in person, with `github_logins` the logins of the GitHub accounts they linked, as each was when linked, sorted, `[]` for none, and `github_link` whether this host can link one (`GET /api/v1/github/link`, #2228), for the header that replaces the banner: it is on the fleet read because that is the dashboard's first call and a second round trip to learn who you are would show the page with a blank header; `role` ∈ `member \| responder \| admin`; on a demo host `viewer` is the anonymous actor · `GET /api/v1/preferences` (row 219) answers a `viewer` too and answers it as `{email, role}`: one concept projected per route, a header needs a name and the settings page needs the address to match against its lists, and no consumer is handed both — each row states its own shape so neither has to be read to understand the other · **`tiles` are org-wide for an admin and count only the caller's team apps (`contributor` or above) for everyone else; no filter narrows either** — an admin's accusation is about the org, not about the view they happen to be looking at, so `secrets_exposed` as a fleet-wide alarm is an admin's instrument, and nobody is accused about apps they cannot see: `secrets_exposed` projects whose latest verdict carries a blocking `secrets.*` finding, `blocked` projects whose `latest.stage` is `blocked`, `approved` projects whose `latest.stage` is `approved` — blocking findings an admin let through, counted apart so they never fall silently out of `blocked` into nothing (a live key approved as a test key stays in front of the admin, in this tile and in `secrets_exposed`) — `never_reviewed` projects **no run has ever looked at** — a project fact (`gate_state`), never a stage: a newer package sitting in `awaiting_review` on a project that has had runs shows that word in its Latest cell and nothing else, no tile tick and no D5 sentence · `failed` projects whose `latest.stage` is `failed` — a run that ended `failed` with any reason, which the newest version has not got past (**amended 2026-10-05**, the fleet data: a failed app was in no tile) · `unowned` projects with an empty `owners[]` · `refused` projects with an egress request of either env that the env **refuses now** (design/EGRESS.md §3.4 and §8.3; **added 2026-11-08**, #2723: `needs_you` picked such an app, but no tile counted it, so the fleet's *Needs you* said nothing needed the reader): the request is `pending`; the env's mode, its own else the org's, refuses the destination as *The check* decides it (`egresscontrol.Refusal`: the mode is `allowlist` and neither an entry of the env's allowlist nor a platform destination allows it); the env's running deployment goes through Canyon, its deploy row's `egress.via` `canyon` (one under `off`, a recreated network's among them, calls out directly, and nothing refuses it); and a call of the version the env runs now was refused: the request's `last_refused_at` at or after the deploy step started the running deployment's container (its deploy row's `container_started_at`, the moment before its `/run`, written by every deploy Canyon runs: a deploy, a promotion, a patch and a rollback; else `run_started_at`, else `deployed_at` on a row from before those columns), less the promote receipt's margin, 90 s (`egressSeenMargin`, *The promote receipt*), since a version calls out as it starts, before its deploy row says it went live (**amended 2026-11-08**, #2723: anchored on `deployed_at`, a call made at boot, an OIDC discovery document's fetch, was no need; **amended 2026-11-09**, #2738: anchored on the run's start, the outgoing version's refusals during a preview's review and build, minutes, counted as the new version's); stricter, never looser, and an env with nothing deployed refuses none. A request stays `pending` after the mode lets its name through or after a deploy of a version that no longer calls it, and is then no need: one predicate, the server's, decides it for this tile, `needs_you` and the app's egress read's `refusing`, so the three never disagree. A person allows or dismisses it on the app's Outbound card, and like every tile it counts only rows the caller reads whole — their contributors', owners' and admins' (design/PERMISSIONS.md, *Outbound*), never a read-only member's; a project counts in every tile it qualifies for · **`needs_you`** (**added 2026-11-08**, #2724), beside the seven and no tile of them: how many needs (*What needs a person*) the rows of this read carry, every page's, whatever the filters, and `org_needs` — the count the rail and the browser tab show, its list the rows `needs_you=true` selects · **every tile of the seven is a filter**: it links to `?tile=<key>`, which selects exactly the apps that tile counted · **`stats` counts the same apps the tiles count, and no filter, `scope` or `sort` narrows it** (**amended 2026-10-05**, the fleet data: counted in the browser from one page, the top block was wrong past 200 apps): `{apps, owners, production, preview, passed, deploys_7d, production_7d}` — `apps` how many, `owners` the distinct people with an `owner` grant on any of them (an `email` grant's address, compared case-insensitively;
